| UK Sanctions List (`uk_sanctions`) | `connectors/uk.ts`, one XML file | 1,685 records | Open Government Licence v3.0 | Entities only (an entry whose kind is missing or unknown is one unreadable entry, so the download fails as `parse_errors` and does not delist); each name a name key (weak), the host of each website a domain key (strong, against the vendor's verified domain; none for an e-mail address in the field or for a host where many have pages), each business registration number an `lei:` or `regno:` key; the UN reference number of a designation the UN made, shown in its text and identifiers (no key) | The list's page with `?uid=` |
| EU consolidated list of financial sanctions (`eu_fsf`) | `connectors/eu.ts`, the XML 1.1 file that the operator's RSS feed names | 1,797 records | Commission reuse notice (Decision 2011/833/EU); CC BY 4.0 | Enterprises only; each alias a name key (weak; a record is titled by its first alias in English or in no language), the host of each website in the contact details a domain key (strong; an e-mail address gives none), each identification number an `lei:` or `regno:` key; the UN reference number (`unitedNationId`, filled for few entries), shown in its text (no key) | The dataset's page with `?logicalId=` |
| National Vulnerability Database (`nvd`) | `connectors/nvd.ts`, read through: the critical CVEs (CVSS 9.0 or more) of the last 119 days for the vendor's CPE vendor token, in any part of a CPE name (applications, operating systems and firmware, hardware) | Asked at run time; an answer is kept 6 hours | Public domain; the NVD's notice is shown | Each CVE, a `cpe:` key (strong); a CVE an answer marks Rejected is stored rejected, and a CVE of the window that a complete answer lacks loses the key | The CVE's page on `nvd.nist.gov` |
| SEC EDGAR filings (`sec`) | `connectors/sec.ts`, read through: the vendor's Form 8-K filings under Item 1.05, the newest three | Asked at run time; an answer is kept 1 hour | Free to reuse (SEC) | Each filing, a `cik:` key: strong for the CIK stored on the vendor, weak for the one registrant whose name is the vendor's (Q3) | The filing on `www.sec.gov` |
| CVE Program records (`cve`) | `connectors/cve.ts`, the daily deltas of cvelistV5 (a zip of JSON records per day, an asset of a release on GitHub), at most seven days a request of the GitHub API | 36,067 records | CVE Terms of Use; MITRE's copyright designation and the licence are stored with the copy | The records published in the last 24 months whose highest CVSS base score is 9.0 or more, and every rejected record; the vendor of each affected product a `cve:` key (strong) | The record on `www.cve.org` |
| UN Security Council Consolidated List (`un_sanctions`), behind Q6 (closed) | `connectors/un.ts`, one XML file | None (its gate is closed) | Personal, non-commercial use only (Q6); the gate stays closed | Entities only; each name and alias a name key (weak) | The list's page with the entry's reference number |
| US Consolidated Screening List (`csl`) | `connectors/csl.ts`: the JSON file `consolidated.json` on `data.trade.gov` (about 34 MB), a conditional request on `ETag` and `Last-Modified`; `download` parses it once and returns a projection of the entries that are kept, so that the hash does not change with the time the file was made | 5,169 records | None published; a work of the US Government (17 U.S.C. §105) compiled by the International Trade Administration, whose developer terms allow services that search, display and analyze it; accepted on 2026-11-07 (Q33). Attribution: `This product uses data from the International Trade Administration's Consolidated Screening List but is not endorsed or certified by the International Trade Administration. Entries adapted by VerifyWise.` | One record per list and name: the lists of Commerce and State (every entry) and of Treasury (the entities that the SDN list does not hold), without the SDN and Palestinian Legislative Council lists; a Denied Persons order past its end day is left out. The list, the name and up to 6 other names, up to 6 countries, the programs, the day listed, the Federal Register notice, the licence requirement and policy and the remarks (each cut and marked `[shortened]`), and the closing sentence that the list is an aid to screening and not the official list. Each name a name key (weak), each website a domain key, each LEI and registration number a key (strong, a number only beside the name). A state, offered as of the copy. Never kept: street, city, postal code, dates and places of birth, citizenship, gender, title, passport and national ID numbers, vessel fields, the file's own ids | The list's page on `www.trade.gov`, with the list's short id and the compact name as query parameters |

Never kept: the people harmed, the editors' ids and notes, the data sources, the taxonomy columns, any report text | The incident's page, `https://incidentdatabase.ai/cite/<id>` |
| MITRE ATLAS case studies (`atlas`) | `connectors/atlas.ts`: the YAML file of the newest format 6 release that `dist/manifest.yaml` of `mitre-atlas/atlas-data` names (a patch release such as `2025.11.2` counts; read with the `yaml` package, 2.9.1, a direct dependency, under the connector's own guards) | 77 records | Apache License 2.0 (Q32). Attribution: `MITRE ATLAS™ case studies, Copyright 2021-<year> The MITRE Corporation, under the Apache License 2.0. Adapted by VerifyWise.`, with the current UTC year, read at each use | One record per case study, an Incident or an Exercise with the type spelled out: id, name, type, target, the actor when it is clearly no natural person (a description such as `Unknown Threat Actor`, or an organization; a name, a handle or anything the rule is unsure of is left out), start date (the year or the month only, where ATLAS dates the case study no more closely), the first paragraph of the summary (cleaned, 600 characters at most) and the number of references, the text kept to 1,300 characters and the title to 200, with no `<` or `>`, as the kit's records; a start date of year 0000 is unreadable; the target is the name key (weak). References, relationships and procedure steps are not kept | The case study's page, `https://atlas.mitre.org/studies/<id>` |
| NHTSA Standing General Order crash reports (`nhtsa_sgo`) | `connectors/nhtsa.ts`: the three current CSV files (ADS, ADAS and OTHER, whose reports do not say which automation was engaged, so its records read "automation not identified") on `static.nhtsa.gov`, downloaded whole with no conditional request | 46 records | None published; a work of the US Government (17 U.S.C. §105); accepted on 2026-11-05 (Q31). Attribution: `NHTSA Standing General Order crash reports, US Department of Transportation. Each program name a name key (weak); the host of the policy a domain key (strong, against the vendor's verified domain) only when its address is used by exactly one row, the host is an apex domain, it is no shared host and no bug-bounty or document platform, and no other record carries the same host (a host that two records would carry is the key of neither, and each keeps its name keys). A program name of fewer than four letters and digits gives no name key, and an organization with no key of either kind is no record and is not counted as unreadable. Dropped before anything is stored: rows whose policy address is shared by 50 or more rows, and dead policies. A row with no program name or no policy address, or with a name of more than 80 characters or an address of more than 2,048, is counted as unreadable, and neither is ever cut. A record's id is its compact name and its host (`@none` without one); a key of more than 200 characters gives an id of its first 183 characters, a tilde and the first 16 hex digits of the SHA-256 of the whole key. A party with a name of more than 120 characters, or of fewer than four letters and digits once its legal form is off, is dropped. A vendor cell is read to 400 characters: a cell that was cut is read only up to its last semicolon, line break, slash or the word and, and the name that the cut ended is no name (a cut cell with no such separator names no vendor); a row whose vendor cell is longer than a megabyte, or whose agency is not an abbreviation of one to six capital letters, is unreadable and adds nothing, not even its agency. | Colombia SIC consumer protection sanctions (`co_sic_penalties`) | `connectors/coSicPenalties.ts`: the Socrata API of `www.datos.gov.co` for the dataset `73dx-n59j`: a count, then the rows (up to 50,000), 1 second apart, both asked in full every time with no validators; the list is the list only when its rows are exactly the count (when they differ, the count is asked once more after a pause: a count that now agrees is taken, and otherwise the download fails as unreachable, `fetch_failed`, which the worker tries again), it has at least 3,000 rows (the count is below 50,000) and its earliest sanction is of 2014-12-31 or before, and a list that is not whole fails the download and never gives part of itself | 2,218 records | CC BY-SA 4.0 (https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by-sa/4.0/), as datos.gov.co states for dataset 73dx-n59j; MinTIC portal terms: cite 'Fuente: Portal de Datos Abiertos www.datos.gov.co' with the data's update date and do not distort the meaning; rows naming natural persons are not copied; adapted records are shared under CC BY-SA 4.0; decided on delegation on 2026-11-10 (Q47). Attribution: `Fuente: Portal de Datos Abiertos www.datos.gov.co (Superintendencia de Industria y Comercio, consumer protection sanctions), CC BY-SA 4.0. Adapted by VerifyWise; the adapted records are shared under CC BY-SA 4.0.` | One record per sanctioned company: the number of sanctions (rows), the first and last year, the total of the fines in Colombian pesos and the newest 3 sanctions. An event (`consumer_sanction_summary`), dated by its newest sanction. The party without its `EN LIQUIDACION` and `CON SIGLA` tails, the whole party, the acronym after `CON SIGLA` and each side of ` Y/O `, of a slash, or of a hyphen or a dash with a blank on one side at least, that has a Spanish legal form are name keys (weak, 6 at most); the data has no tax number, website or address. The party is judged for natural persons twice before anything is stored (the Spanish rule of `connectors/kit/persons.ts`): as a whole, where a cell with the shape of a person's name drops its row, and side by side, where the cell is split on ` Y/O `, on a hyphen or a dash with a blank on one side at least, on a slash, and on ` Y `, ` E `, ` & ` and ` + ` with a blank on each side, and each side is judged on its own without its tails. A side with the shape of a person's name is removed; the party of the row is the sides that remain, joined by ` Y/O `, and the cell that names the person is never a name, an id, a display, a title, a part of the text, an identifier or a key. The row is dropped, and counted nowhere, when no side is left or none that remains can be matched by a vendor (four letters and digits once its legal form is off, and a word that is no legal form, no word of an organization, no particle and no word of a family firm). A party of more than 300 characters once cleaned, or once its persons are out, is unreadable and never cut; the title and the text show at most 120 characters of it, cut and marked, and the body and the identifiers hold it whole. Never kept: the system columns (`:id`, `:created_at`, `:version`) and the time of `:updated_at` (only its day is kept, in the citation of the portal that closes the text) | The dataset's page on `www.datos.gov.co`, with `?party=` and the company's id |

A state (`algorithm_register_supplier`), offered as of the copy. Each spelling of the supplier (10 at most) a name key (weak); the Companies House number a `regno:` key (only when the cell names one firm, accepted or not: a placeholder, an address line, a country, the deploying body, and a part of one character, with no letter or with an `@`, do not count, nor does a part the accept rule refuses that is a web address, a host or a bare domain in lower case (a host written with a capital anywhere, `Quillon.ai`, names a firm and counts unless it is a supplier's own), a detail glued with a dot (`Tel.02079460000`, `GOV.UK`), the details of a firm alone (`Phone 020 7946 0000`), a clause in lower case that names no firm, a label of details whose text after the colon is one of these, or the details of a supplier of the cell, and the names around a web address in a part, and a firm in the bracket at the end of a part that does not count as a name of its own, count only when no supplier of the cell has them (see the changes of 2026-11-12 below); strong only beside the name; a number of seven digits gets its dropped leading zero back, so that `1234567` is shown as `01234567`, and a number that begins with a zero is keyed with it and without it, `regno:01234567` and `regno:1234567`, since a vendor's number is keyed as written), only when the record names exactly one supplier and its section of the number holds exactly one number, and no limit cut either section. A name is a supplier only when it passes the rule of the connector (2 to 80 characters and at most 7 words, no sentence, no postcode, no e-mail address, no number alone, no country of the United Kingdom, and not the deploying body itself; an ampersand at either end is dropped, so `PolyAI &` is PolyAI; the lines of a postal address that a UK postcode closes are no suppliers: walking back from the postcode, the countries, at most one town and the lines that begin with a house number or end in a street or building word, never the first line of the block, which is the name), and a name of more than 120 characters once folded gives no row and is never cut. A result with no text, or whose address is no record's page of the list, is unreadable; a result that names no supplier, a record laid out without numbered headings (version 1.1 of the standard) among them, is read and gives no row, which is a gap of the list and not an error. Never kept: the contact e-mail addresses and named officials of the record's text, the linked documents, and the function, region and organization type | The page of the supplier's newest record on `www.gov.uk` |

The record says that GLEIF is not associated with VerifyWise, and that a lapsed registration means the data were not renewed on time. A state (`lei_record`), dated by the last update. The one key is the `lei:` of the vendor's identifier (strong); a name never matches. Never kept: addresses, the BIC, MIC, OCID, QCC, GEM and S&P numbers, the issuer and how it validated the entity, the number in the entity's own register, other names, successor and associated entities, and links | The LEI's record at GLEIF, `https://api.gleif.org/api/v1/lei-records/<LEI>` |

- **The tables.** Four shared tables without `organization_id`, which hold no tenant data and are written only by the load of the bundles and the lookup (`vrdb_*` in the `sharedTables` of `scripts/auditTenantIsolationCoverage.ts`): `vrdb_sources`, one row per list, upserted from `vrdb/sources.ts` at every load, with the state of its copy (the last attempt and success, a fixed error code and never a message, the validators of a download, which a load from a bundle leaves null, the number of active records; staleness is computed from `last_success_at`, never stored); `vrdb_records`, one row per record of a list with its typed match keys (`name:…`, `kev:…`, `domain:…`) under a GIN index, where a record that left its list keeps its row with another status; `vrdb_sync_runs` (named for the syncs of earlier builds), one row per load of a bundle that was new, with the SHA-256 of the bundle file, kept 90 days (`LOAD_RUNS_KEPT_DAYS` in `vrdb/loadBundled.ts`); and `vrdb_lookups`, the state of each key of a read-through source (the key, when it was last answered, `fetched_at`, null for a key whose first answer failed; when it may be asked again, `expires_at`; the outcome and the number of records). A partial index on the ids of rejected records serves the lookup's filter of rejected CVEs (`20261031031802-vrdb-read-through.js`). The raw files of a list are never kept, by the server or in a bundle: a bundle holds the parsed records. One tenant-scoped table, `vendor_vrdb_matches`, holds the records that a vendor of an organization matched, with the method and the strength of the match; it is in the tenant-isolation registry (`vendor-vrdb-matches.isolation.test.ts`). The queries are in `utils/vrdb.utils.ts`; the migration is `20261030223825-vendor-risk-database.js`.
- **The bundles** (`vrdb/bundle.ts`). The server downloads none of the lists. Each release ships one file per list under `Servers/data/vrdb/`, `<id>.json.gz`: gzipped JSON of the records as the list's connector parsed them, with the format (1), the list's id, the connector's `version`, `builtAt` (when the list was downloaded), the list's own version when it states one, for the CVE records the cursor (the last day the bundle holds in full), and the records sorted by id. The gzip header carries no time, so two builds of the same records differ only by their `builtAt`. `manifest.json` beside them lists each bundle with its `builtAt`, its number of records, its connector version, its source version and the SHA-256 of its bytes, so that a release diff shows which lists changed. The directory is found beside the sources or the build (`bundleDir`), and `VRDB_BUNDLE_DIR` overrides it. A list is as current as the release that carries it, and its records say so: they are dated by `builtAt`, not by the day they were loaded. The bundles of 2026-11-23 hold 31 lists, about 7.3 MB in all (the CVE records: 36,067 records in 3.5 MB). `eu_dg_comp` has none: the Commission's open-data files no longer carry `caseInitiationDate`, so its connector refuses them as not the list, and the list is not checked (`bundle_missing`) until the connector is updated and a bundle is built. Maintainers build the bundles before a release (see [Refreshing the lists for a release](#refreshing-the-lists-for-a-release)).
- **The load** (`loadBundledSources` in `vrdb/loadBundled.ts`). At every start the backend (`jobs/producer.ts`) calls `scheduleVendorRiskDatabaseLoad` (`vrdb/queue.ts`). It removes the per-list schedulers that builds before the bundles registered (`vrdb_sync_<id>`), so that no upgraded deployment keeps downloading, and, while `VENDOR_RESEARCH_RECORDS` is not `false`, queues one job `vrdb_load` on the queue `vendor-risk-database`, deduplicated by the id `vrdb_load`, so that the starts of several replicas give one load while one waits or runs. The worker of `vrdb/worker.ts`, in the process of the other workers (`jobs/worker.ts`), takes one job at a time, checks the switch again (an operator may have turned the records off after the job was queued), and loads each list the gates allow, one after another (`activeMirrors`). For each list it writes the definition row (`upsertVrdbSourceQuery`) and reads the bundle. A bundle whose SHA-256 is the snapshot hash of the list's last successful load is the one already loaded and is left alone, so a restart writes nothing. A new bundle is applied with the same store queries that the syncs of earlier builds used. A list taken whole is written in one transaction (`applyVrdbSnapshotQuery`): a new record is inserted active, a changed one takes its new content, one that had left the list is active again, and an active record that the bundle no longer holds becomes `withdrawn` (the catalog, the AI lists and every copy added on 2026-11-10 but two) or `delisted` (a sanctions list, the Consolidated Screening List, Canada's sanctions list and the FedRAMP Marketplace; `missingStatus` in `vrdb/sources.ts`). The CVE records, whose `missingStatus` is null, are applied as changes (`applyVrdbDeltaQuery`) in batches of 1,000 records and ids (`DELTA_BATCH`): the bundle's records, and the ids that the copy holds with keys (or not active) and the bundle lacks (`listVrdbKeyedRecordIdsQuery`), which are retired: they lose their keys, so no run is offered them. Only the last batch sets the cursor and notes the success, so a load that stops halfway is applied again at the next start. The records and the list's `last_success_at` are dated by the bundle's `builtAt`, not by the time of the load. A list whose bundle is missing or cannot be read is marked failed with a fixed code (`bundle_missing`, `bundle_unreadable`, `bundle_wrong_format` or `bundle_wrong_list`) and keeps its copy; a load that fails in the database finishes its run row as failed (`load_failed`), and the other lists are loaded all the same. BullMQ tries a failed load job three times with an exponential back-off from one minute (`VRDB_JOB_OPTIONS`). A load that changed the status of a record adds the status job of its list (below). With `VENDOR_RESEARCH_RECORDS=false` no load is queued and a queued one loads nothing.
- **Staleness.** A bundled list is stale 60 days after its bundle was built (`BUNDLED_STALE_AFTER_HOURS` in `vrdb/sources.ts`, read through `staleAfterHoursOf`): each release carries new bundles, so a list that old is on a deployment that has not been upgraded for two months. Its records still count, and the screen says the copy is not up to date. A read-through source keeps its own hours: its answer is stale 48 hours after it was fetched, and is kept 6 hours (the NVD), 1 hour (the SEC) or 24 hours (GLEIF) before the source is asked again. The definitions of the lists carry no `schedule` and no `staleAfterHours` of their own.
- **The match** (`vrdb/keys.ts`), with no model. The vendor's keys are each of its names and other names, compacted as written and without the legal form, and the brand of its domain, as a KEV key and as the vendor of a CVE record's affected product (`cve:`); the vendor part of its CPE names (`cpe:`); each of its names and other names (never the brand) of four characters or more as a name key, since a sanctions list and an AI list are matched by names; each domain that the website check verified as the vendor's (`verifiedDomainsOf` in `preflight.ts`: the recorded domain when its home page was read and names the vendor, with the domain it moved to when that is the vendor's too); and the keys of its [identifiers](#create-vendor): its LEI (`lei:`), its registration number (`regno:`, six letters and digits or more) and its CIK (`cik:`). Its names, its LEI and its registration number give their keys twice, as written and folded as the list kit folds the names of its records (NFKC, the characters nobody sees out: `lineWithin` of `kit/clean.ts`), so that a name typed in NFD or in full-width letters matches the records of the kit's lists, and the lists before the kit, whose keys are of the text as written, still match it. A record's keys are the names, KEV vendor, CPE or CVE vendor, website hosts and numbers that its list gives (a number that passes the LEI check is an `lei:` key, any other with a digit a `regno:` key, `identifierKeysOf`), and a record is the vendor's when the two share a key, the surest first (`matchOf`): an LEI, a verified domain, a stored CIK, a CPE, KEV or CVE vendor names the vendor the way the list does and is a strong match; a registration number is strong only when the record also shares a name with the vendor, since a number is unique only in its register; a CIK found by the vendor's name (Q3) and a name alone are weak (this may be another company). A host where many have pages (`sharedHosts.ts`: the platforms of the registry where users publish, and code hosts, site builders, app hosts, `t.me`, `vk.com` and `linktr.ee`, and the hosts where unrelated parties publish code, models, packages, documents and apps (`bitbucket.org`, `codeberg.org`, `sourceforge.net`, `gitee.com`, `githubusercontent.com`, `huggingface.co`, `kaggle.com`, `replicate.com`, `npmjs.com`, `pypi.org`, `crates.io`, `rubygems.org`, `nuget.org`, `packagist.org`, `pkg.go.dev`, `hub.docker.com`, `docs.google.com`, `drive.google.com`, `notion.so`, `readthedocs.org`, `apps.apple.com`, `play.google.com`, `chromewebstore.google.com`, `marketplace.visualstudio.com`; a per-user subdomain on `bitbucket.io`, `codeberg.page`, `gitbook.io`, `hf.space` or `readthedocs.io` stays the user's own site), with the hosts under them) is never a key, on either side, and its brand is never the vendor's (`records/match.ts`): a LinkedIn page or `acme.github.io` says nothing about who it is.
- **The lookup** (`createVrdbLookup`, `offerRecords` and `sharedVrdbLookup` in `vrdb/lookup.ts`). `gatherSources` (`gather.ts`) asks it beside the web lookups of the other databases (Have I Been Pwned and the status page). The keys are matched against the copies in two queries; only active records are offered, the strong matches first, then the newest; a catalog entry older than the lookback of its category is left out and counted as old (a designation, a listing of the Consolidated Screening List (`restricted_party`) and an authorization of the FDA list are states, so the lookback does not apply to them; an AI incident, an ATLAS case study, a summary of crash reports and a recall of an AI-enabled device (`ai_device_recall`) are events, and it does apply to them, by their own day: `isEvent` in `vrdb/lookup.ts`; of the lists of 2026-11-10, the 12 that record matters are events, dated by their newest matter, and the others, Canada's sanctions entries and the registers and status lists, are states: see **The third round of lists** below); at most 5 records of the catalog, 3 of the NVD, of the SEC, of the CVE records, of ATLAS, of NHTSA, of the FDA recalls and of the AI Incident Database, and 2 of each sanctions list, of the Consolidated Screening List and of the FDA list are offered (`MAX_RECORDS`; of the lists of 2026-11-10, 2 of ten and 1 of the other thirteen), the rest counted as more; a CVE that one list offered is not offered again by a later one (the catalog, then the NVD, then the CVE records), and a vulnerability whose CVE any list rejected is never offered. A record's text is the list's own, without the characters nobody sees (format and tag characters, variation selectors: `withoutHiddenCharacters` in `text.ts`, taken out again when a stored record is offered), the lookup note for a weak match (a name key, or a CIK found by the vendor's name; added when the record is offered, so the stored text is unchanged), and the day of the copy ("As of …"). A list with no copy yet is `failed`, and the screen names it under "Not checked"; a copy older than the hours of its list (`staleAfterHoursOf` in `vrdb/sources.ts`: 60 days after its bundle was built for a bundled list, 48 hours after its answer for a read-through source) is stale, and its records are offered all the same. For the CVE records, which are read by their changes, the day of the copy is the end of the last day the bundle holds (its cursor) when that is earlier than its last success (`copyUpTo` in `vrdb/lookup.ts`): a bundle holds the changes up to the day before it was built, so its records read as of that day. The day of each record, the staleness and the coverage's day all follow it. `gatherSources` gives the lookup the verified domains of the website check (`verifiedDomains`). The matches of the offered records are kept in `vendor_vrdb_matches`. When the database cannot be read the run goes on without the records, and every list is `failed`. With `VENDOR_RESEARCH_RECORDS=false` the lookup reads nothing and every list is `off`. A list behind a closed gate is neither looked up nor in the coverage.
- **The read-through** (`vrdb/readThrough.ts`, spec D4.8 step 3). A read-through source is asked at run time by a key of the vendor (`keyOf` of its connector: the NVD by the CPE vendor token, the SEC by the stored CIK, else by the registrant whose name is the vendor's, GLEIF by the stored LEI and by nothing else, so that a vendor with no stored LEI has no key and the source is `not_researched` for it; every SEC request names `VerifyWise tech@verifywise.ai` in its User-Agent), and its answer is stored in the tables of the copies: the records in `vrdb_records`, the state of the key in `vrdb_lookups`. An answer younger than the source's hours (6 for the NVD, 1 for the SEC, 24 for GLEIF) is not asked again, by any run of any organization. An expired or new key is asked within the run's 8 seconds (`READ_THROUGH_TIMEOUT_MS`), on a turn of the source's spacer, which all the workers share in Redis (`records/sharedSpacer.ts`: the NVD 4 requests in 30 seconds, waiting 3 seconds at most; the SEC 5 a second, waiting 2 seconds at most; GLEIF 50 requests in 60 seconds, waiting 1.5 seconds at most); the records of the answer are inserted or brought up to date in one transaction with the key's state (`saveReadThroughAnswerQuery`). An answer that holds its whole window (the NVD's: its `totalResults` fits the page of 50; the connector's `coversSince`) takes the key off a stored record of the key published in that window that it lacks: the CVE was rejected, re-scored below critical or moved off the vendor, so it is no longer offered for the key; the record keeps its status and its other keys, and a pending finding that cites it keeps its label (no status changed, so the status job does not run). A record published before the window aged out of it and stays, offered as of the last answer that held it (`retrieved_at`). A CVE an answer marks Rejected is stored rejected; since the query asks for critical CVEs, a rejected CVE without a critical score simply leaves the answer. The SEC's answers cover no window: a filing is never taken back. A failure keeps the stored records in use, makes the source `failed` in the coverage, and leaves the key alone for 5 minutes (`RETRY_AFTER_FAILURE_MS`), unless the run stopped or the turn was too far off, which says the source is busy, not down. The coverage of a read-through source is that of a list, dated by its answer, and the screen lists it as one.
- **The gates** (`vrdb/gates.ts`). A list that waits for a person is defined, tested and documented, but while its gate is closed it is never bundled, loaded or looked up, and no run shows it (the bundler and the load both take `activeMirrors`). A gate opens by a change of that file, once the question it waits for is decided. **Q4**, the SEC: decided on 2026-10-31, and the gate is open. The User-Agent that the SEC asks of automated access names the e-mail address of whoever runs the client, so that the SEC can write before it limits or blocks the client. The address is `tech@verifywise.ai`, the Technical Support contact that verifywise.ai/contact publishes (`SEC_CONTACT`; a role and not a person, and a constant and not a setting, so that every deployment declares the same identity), and every SEC request, the read-through, the text of a filing and the header of a filing alike, carries `VerifyWise tech@verifywise.ai`. **Q6**, the UN list: the written permission of the United Nations (`UN_PERMISSION_GRANTED`). Its terms of use, read on 2026-10-31, allow personal, non-commercial use only, so the gate stays closed; the element names of `connectors/un.ts` were checked against the real file the same day (274 entities, no error). The UN's designations reach a run through the UK and EU lists all the same: on 2026-10-30 the UK list held every entity of the UN list, 269 of the 274 with their UN reference number, which a UK or EU record shows. **Q7**, the CVE records: decided on 2026-10-31, and the gate is open. The CVE Terms of Use for this use were accepted by the product owner on 2026-10-31, without a separate legal review, and the product owner permitted the download of the baseline file (`CVE_TERMS_APPROVED`). Q5, the OFAC list, was accepted by the product owner on 2026-10-31 as well (it is not a gate: the list publishes no terms, as a work of the US Government). **Q29**, the AI Incident Database: decided on 2026-11-05 to build the list behind a closed gate (`AIID_PERMISSION_GRANTED`, false). The collections of its weekly export that the database reads (incidents, entities and classifications) are licensed CC BY-SA 4.0, but the site's general terms bar anyone from selling or commercially exploiting any aspect of the site without express permission, so the licence and the terms disagree on their face, and the operator, the Responsible AI Collaborative, names a contact for such questions (info@incidentdatabase.ai). The gate opens on the operator's written confirmation that a commercial product may store the export and show records derived from it under CC BY-SA 4.0, or on the product owner's explicit written acceptance of that risk. **To open it,** a maintainer sets `AIID_PERMISSION_GRANTED` to `true` in `vrdb/gates.ts`, builds its bundle (`npm run vrdb:bundle -- --only aiid`, see [Refreshing the lists for a release](#refreshing-the-lists-for-a-release)) and ships both in a release: at its next start a deployment loads the bundle, and from then on the list is in the coverage of the runs (under "Not checked" while no bundle of it has been loaded). Before it opens, a reviewer re-reads 20 random records for the names of private individuals, because the editors' descriptions name victims and the accused. The same review looks at the developer and deployer lines and at the match keys: the connector drops kinds of party but not names of people, and the export of 2026-11-02 names some public figures there; the review decides whether such a name may stay or a person-name filter is built first. **Q31**, the NHTSA crash reports, and **Q32**, MITRE ATLAS, were accepted on 2026-11-05 under the product owner's delegation and are not gates: NHTSA publishes no licence and its files are a work of the US Government (17 U.S.C. §105), accepted in the way of Q5, and the LICENSE file of the ATLAS data repository, the Apache License 2.0, governs over the README's release marking; the repository has no NOTICE file (checked on 2026-11-05), so section 4(d) of the licence adds nothing to the attribution. **Q33**, the US Consolidated Screening List, and **Q34**, the FDA recalls, were accepted on 2026-11-07 under the product owner's delegation and are not gates. The Consolidated Screening List publishes no licence: it is a compilation by the International Trade Administration (ITA) of lists that the Departments of Commerce, State and Treasury publish, a work of the US Government (17 U.S.C. §105), accepted in the way of Q5 and Q31 without a separate legal review; ITA's developer terms allow a service to search, display and analyze it and ask for a notice, which the attribution gives in an adapted form because the database reads the downloadable file and not the Data API that the notice names; the terms bar modifying the content while naming ITA as its source, so every record says that it is adapted. openFDA's device recall data is CC0 1.0 (credit requested, not required), and the GMDN content of its `openfda` block, licensed separately, is never read or stored. **Q35 to Q53**, the lists of 2026-11-10 (see **The third round of lists** below), were decided on 2026-11-10 under the product owner's delegation, and none is a gate: each list is built open. **Q35** (the NHTSA defect investigations) and **Q36** (the NHTSA recalls) were accepted in the way of Q31, as works of the US Government that NHTSA publishes with no licence: the investigation summaries, which name petitioners, the manufacturer-supplied fields 25 to 27 of the recalls file and its Part 573 texts are never stored. **Q37**, the FDA device enforcement reports (CC0 1.0), is a fold into the FDA recalls (Q34) and no list of its own: only the class and the classification day of a recalled product are carried, and the licence and attribution of the `fda_device_recalls` row name both endpoints. **Q38**, the PipeRoll registry (CC BY 4.0; the MIT licence of its repository covers the tooling), never reads its `aiid_incident_id` field, so that nothing of the AI Incident Database (Q29) passes through it. **Q39**, the AI Hallucination Cases database (CC BY 4.0), carries a Content-Signal `ai-train=no` in its operator's `robots.txt`: its data never trains or fine-tunes a model, and a record holds counts per tool and no party, court or case text. **Q40**, the Government of Canada AI Register, takes the licence of its dataset record on `open.canada.ca` (OGL-Canada 2.0), not the terms of the Canada.ca pages. **Q41**, the US federal AI use case inventory, is a work of the US Government accepted in the way of Q31 and Q33. **Q42**, the Dutch Algorithm Register, names no licence: its copyright page permits reuse of its content, which is a new precedent. **Q43** (Epoch AI's data on AI models, a Creative Commons Attribution licence), **Q44** (the Foundation Model Transparency Index, CC BY 4.0, shown as a count of the published file's own cells and never as the index's score), **Q45** (DG Competition's case data under the Commission reuse notice: the antitrust, cartel and Digital Markets Act files only, with no attachment or press release and no person's name), **Q46** (Canada's sanctions list: the OGL-Canada licence of the dataset record is taken as the specification that the Canada.ca terms reserve, and only entities are copied), **Q47** (Colombia's SIC sanctions, CC BY-SA 4.0, so the adapted records are shared under it), **Q48** (the FedRAMP Marketplace, a work of the US General Services Administration: only its status fields), **Q49** (the disclose.io program list, CC0 1.0 per its LICENSE and attributed as CC BY 4.0 per its README), **Q50** (the California Attorney General's breach list, in the public domain by its conditions page, without the sample notices), **Q51** (the UK algorithmic transparency records, of which 13 of 155 come from bodies that are not Crown bodies and rely on GOV.UK's statement that its content is under the OGL v3.0), **Q52** (the CMA cases, the OFSI notices and the MHRA lists, OGL v3.0) and **Q53** (GLEIF, CC0 1.0 under terms that bar implying an association with GLEIF) were accepted on conditions that the licence and attribution strings in the table above record. **Q39 and Q42 are built open on delegation, for the product owner's confirmation:** if the owner reverses either, the list gets a gate, as the AI Incident Database did (Q29), and its copy is deleted.
- **The AI lists** (Q30 to Q32, 2026-11-05; spec sections D2.14 to D2.18 and D4.12). Four lists about AI enter the database as copies, in the category AI-specific risks (`ai_specific`): the MITRE ATLAS case studies (`atlas`), NHTSA's crash reports (`nhtsa_sgo`), the FDA list of AI-enabled medical devices (`fda_ai_devices`) and the AI Incident Database (`aiid`, behind the gate Q29). Each copy is stale 60 days after its bundle was built, and a record that its list no longer holds becomes `withdrawn`. A fifth list, the FDA recalls of the devices on the FDA list (`fda_device_recalls`), was added on 2026-11-07 (below).
  - **Record types, class and strength.** `ai_incident` (AIID), `ai_case_study` (ATLAS) and `crash_report_summary` (NHTSA) are events: `isEvent` in `vrdb/lookup.ts` holds them with `vulnerability` and `sec_filing`, so the 24-month lookback applies to them by their own day (the incident's day, the start date of a case study, the first day of the newest incident month of the crash reports) and the record is offered with it. `ai_device_authorizations` (FDA) is a state, like a sanctions entry: no lookback, and the record is offered as of the copy. `aiid` is classed unverified (an aggregator of allegations), `atlas` trusted press (MITRE curates the case studies with references), `nhtsa_sgo` and `fda_ai_devices` official; all four are weak, so a record gives `low` support and the name-only note whatever its class.
  - **Granularity and privacy.** NHTSA is summarised per reporting entity and file and not per report (one entity has thousands), and the VIN, location, time, narrative, operating entity and every column of confidential business information are never read into a record. The AIID record leaves out the people harmed, the editors' ids and notes and every report text (the licence excludes them too), and the ATLAS record leaves out references, relationships and procedure steps. The address of an NHTSA or FDA record carries a slug of a company name in a query parameter, never a person, so that no two records share an address.
  - **Hostile text.** The description of an ATLAS case study can carry a prompt-injection payload (some do in the real file). The connector first brings the text to the form it finally reads in: it folds compatibility forms (a full-width `<` is a `<`), removes control characters and characters a reader cannot see, and decodes entities until nothing changes, so that a tag written as entities is a tag (stricter than decoding last, which would keep the text of a block written that way). It removes each `<atlas-example>` block whole (an unclosed one to the end of the text) and starts the pass again when that changed the text, then strips every other tag and keeps the text between them, reduces links to their text and drops `[[reference]]`. It repeats the pass until the text no longer changes, then keeps the first paragraph, cut at a word boundary: 600 characters for the summary, 200 for the name, 300 for the target and for the actor. The actor is shown only when it is clearly no natural person (`atlasActorOf`; the rule fails closed). A text that never settles becomes empty. An AIID description loses its tags and links, is decoded and cut in the same order, and gets the same 600-character cap, with `[shortened]` after a cut one. The YAML of ATLAS is read under the connector's own guards: it is refused when it has an alias, an explicit tag, a repeated key, a second document or more than a million lexemes (the package's check for repeated keys is quadratic, so the connector makes it in linear time). The record then goes through the same escaping and untrusted-data line as every source.
  - **Downloads and limits.** Each list is downloaded only from its own host, through the page guard: `incidentdatabase.ai` (the snapshots page, 2 MiB, 30 s) and the host of its public export bucket (the file, 16 MiB, 120 s, its final address after redirects on that host for a 304 as well as a 200; the zip inside it at most 64 entries, 48 MiB for an entry and 64 MiB in all; the sheet at most 20,000 rows and 256 columns); `raw.githubusercontent.com/mitre-atlas/atlas-data/` (the manifest 256 KiB and the YAML 8 MiB, each of the repository's own path after redirects, a 304 of the YAML included); `static.nhtsa.gov/odi/ffdd/sgo-2021-01/` (three files of 32 MiB and 60,000 reports at most each, about 5.3 MB in all, downloaded whole by each build of the bundles); and `www.fda.gov` (2 MiB; the answer must come from that host and the header row must hold exactly the six columns). The other lists use a conditional request. An address never enters a record, a log line or an error text. A row that cannot be read (no id, no title or name, a date that does not parse) is skipped and counted, the bundler prints how many it left out (`<id>: N entries do not parse and were left out`, never a row) and it fails as `parse_errors` only when they exceed max(10, 1% of the rows); a file that is not the list (a missing column, an unknown format version, no sheet `Incidents`, a YAML that breaks a guard) is `wrong_root`, and a snapshots page that links no export is `feed_without_file`.
  - **Attribution and licence.** The row of each list in `vrdb_sources` carries the licence and the attribution of the table above, and the Data sources line of the coverage shows the attribution of each list the run asked. MITRE's copyright year is the current UTC year, read at each use. The attribution of the AI Incident Database says that the records are adapted by VerifyWise and shared under CC BY-SA 4.0: share-alike applies to the stored and shown record texts, and leaving out the attribution or the share-alike note is a licence breach even where the use itself is allowed.
  - **How to read the coverage row of a list that has no copy yet.** A run's coverage has one row for each active list. `found` and `none` mean that the list had a copy and the run asked it: the screen shows it under "Checked" with its count, and a list with `none` also under "No records in … as of …" with the day of its copy (the 22 lists of 2026-11-10 but Canada's sanctions list are named under "Checked" only when they offered a record, and are counted in its last item when they held none; see **The third round of lists** below). `failed` with no day of copy means that the list has no copy yet: the load of the start has not reached its bundle yet, or the bundle the release ships cannot be read, so a run then shows it under "Not checked" and its findings rest on none of it. That is a gap and not a clean result, and it ends when a bundle of the list is loaded. A list the release ships **no bundle of at all** (not named in `Servers/data/vrdb/manifest.json`; `eu_dg_comp` in the bundles of 2026-11-23) is never loaded in that deployment, so it is not part of a run's coverage: it is neither checked nor not checked, has no row, is not looked up and raises no warning. The rule is `coveredSources` in `vrdb/sources.ts`, given the shipped lists by `shippedBundleIds` (`vrdb/bundle.ts`, which reads the manifest once); `offerRecords` and `createVrdbLookup` (`vrdb/lookup.ts`) both take it. When the manifest cannot be read or names no list, every active list stays in the coverage, so a broken release shows its lists as not checked instead of a screening that looks complete. A run stored before this rule keeps the `failed` row it was stored with. A copy older than the hours of its list (60 days after its bundle was built) is stale: the run asks it all the same, and the screen says "not up to date". `not_researched` (the vendor has no website, so AI-specific risks, the FDA recalls among them, and certifications were not researched; a read-through source that the vendor has no key for, GLEIF without a stored LEI, is `not_researched` as well; the Consolidated Screening List belongs to legal and regulatory matters, which are researched with no website) and `off` (the records are turned off) put the list on no "Checked" or "Not checked" line. The AI Incident Database has no row at all while its gate is closed, so its absence from "Checked" and "Not checked" does not say that no incident names the vendor.
- **The US Consolidated Screening List** (Q33, 2026-11-07; spec sections D2.19 and D4.13). `csl` copies the file that the International Trade Administration publishes at `data.trade.gov` (about 34 MB of JSON): the export control and trade restriction lists of Commerce, State and Treasury, without the SDN list, which `ofac` holds. No gate is involved. The category is legal and regulatory matters, the record type `restricted_party`, a state: a listing in force does not age out, so the lookback does not apply and the record is offered as of the copy. The class is official, and a match by name is weak.
  - **Kept and dropped.** A list is the code in the parentheses of an entry's `source`. Kept: the Entity List, the Denied Persons List, the Unverified List and the Military End User List (Commerce), the ITAR Debarred List and the Nonproliferation Sanctions (State), and the Sectoral Sanctions Identifications, Non-SDN Menu-Based Sanctions, Non-SDN Chinese Military-Industrial Complex Companies, Capta and Foreign Sanctions Evaders lists (Treasury). Dropped: the SDN list (`ofac` holds it) and the Palestinian Legislative Council list (78 individuals, no company). An entry of a list that is not in the map is skipped and counted and is no error, so a new list does not fail the download, and a renamed list that was large fails through the shrink check. The lists of Treasury are typed and keep the entries of type `Entity`; the lists of Commerce and State are not typed and keep every entry. A Denied Persons entry whose `end_date` is before the day of the download (UTC) is dropped. The projection of `download` applies that (with a clock that a test sets) and `parse` never does, so the filter is of the day the bundle is built. An order that expires after that day stays in the copy until the next bundle. An entry whose `entity_number` is that of an SDN entry of the same file is dropped (90 entries of the Sectoral list on 2026-11-07), since `ofac` is the authority for it. The file of 2026-11-07 gave 5,172 records.
  - **Granularity and keys.** One record per list and name (the name compared as letters and digits), so that a name with several orders is one record whose text says how many entries of that name the list holds, and the record does not depend on the file's own ids, which are hashes for the lists other than OFAC's. Each name, other name (100 read at most for an entry) and `Issuer Name` id is a name key (as written and without the legal form, 4 characters or more, 200 at most for a record); the host of each `Website` id is a domain key (strong, as for the UK and EU lists); the ids of the types Legal Entity Number, Registration Number, Registration ID, Company Number, Business Registration Number, Public Registration Number, Unified Social Credit Code (USCC) and Tax ID No. give `lei:` or `regno:` keys (an LEI strong alone, any other number only beside the name). An ISIN, a SWIFT code, a ticker, a national ID number, a passport number, a date, a gender, a title or an address is never a key.
  - **The record and what it leaves out.** The text begins `US Consolidated Screening List (trade.gov).` and holds the list, the name, other names (6 shown: the file's own, then the names that the `Issuer Name` ids of an entry give), countries (6), the programs, the websites (5 shown) and the registration numbers (6 shown) that the record is matched by, the day listed, for the Denied Persons List the day the order ends, the Federal Register notice (200 characters), the licence requirement and the licence policy (300 each) and the remarks (400), and, when the name has more than one entry on the list, how many (the other fields and the match keys are then those of the entry with the newest start date); a cut ends in ` [shortened]`, and the summary is at most 4,000 characters. It ends with the sentence that the list is an aid to screening and not the official list, and that the entry is to be checked against the official publication of the list (the Federal Register for the lists of Commerce and State). The title is `Consolidated Screening List, <list>: <name>`, and the address is `https://www.trade.gov/consolidated-screening-list` with the list's short id and the compact name as the query parameters `list` and `entry`, so that no two records share one. **Never stored:** the street, city, state and postal code of an address, dates and places of birth, citizenships and nationalities, gender, title, passport and national ID numbers, the vessel fields, the file's hashed ids, `standard_order`, `search_performed_at` and `last_imported`. The remarks, the licence fields and the OFAC ids are free text that a model reads, so each is cleaned (compatibility forms folded, control, zero-width and bidirectional characters out, white space collapsed) and cut before the projection is returned, and the record goes through the same escaping and untrusted-data line as every source.
  - **Download and limits.** One file, 64 MiB at most (it is 34 MB), `application/json` and `application/octet-stream`, 180 s, and the final host must be `data.trade.gov` over https or the download fails as `wrong_root`; ITA answers a HEAD request with 404, so the job makes a GET. `download` parses the text once and checks that it is the whole list (an object whose `results` and `sources_used` are arrays, with `offset` 0 and `total` equal to the number of results, since a paged answer is not the list), and returns one body: the kept entries, reduced to the fields above and sorted, with the version of each kept list. The records therefore do not change with the time the file was made (`search_performed_at`) or the `last_imported` of a list, and the 34 MB text and its objects are not held once `download` has returned. At most 20,000 records are accepted (more is `wrong_root`); an entry of a kept list with no name, a name or other name of more than 500 characters, or a field of the wrong JSON type, is skipped and counted up to max(10, 1%) of the entries read, as for the CVE records, and past that the download fails as `parse_errors`; the bundler's shrink check applies. The version of the copy is the newest `source_last_updated` day among the kept lists.
  - **Coverage.** The row of `csl` is as for the other lists. It is researched with legal and regulatory matters, which need no website, so a vendor with none is looked up in it by name. A deployment has no copy until the load of its start has applied the list's bundle: until then the row is `failed` with no day of copy, and the screen lists the list under "Not checked".
- **The FDA recalls** (Q34, 2026-11-07; spec sections D2.20 and D4.13). `fda_device_recalls` copies the recalls of the devices that are on the FDA's list of AI-enabled medical devices (`fda_ai_devices`), from openFDA (`api.fda.gov`, the device recall endpoint), joined to that list by the K and PMA numbers of its submissions. No gate is involved. The category is AI-specific risks and the record type `ai_device_recall`, an event: the 24-month lookback applies by the earliest initiation day of the event, and the record is offered with it. The class is official, and a match by name is weak. The recall endpoint has no class field, so the class of a recall (Class I, II or III) comes from the FDA's device enforcement reports, which the connector reads for the class and the day of the classification and for nothing else (Q37, 2026-11-10); a record of an event that those reports give no class says that the FDA's recall page shows it.
  - **The download is several requests, and gives nothing until every one has succeeded.** (1) A probe of each endpoint, `device/recall.json?limit=1` and `device/enforcement.json?limit=1` (16 MiB, as a join answer, since one product can carry millions of characters of lot numbers; 30 s): its `meta.last_updated` must be a day and its `meta.results.total` above 10,000, or the download fails as `wrong_root`. openFDA answers 404 when a search has no match, and the probes are what lets the join and the class stage read a 404 as "no match" and not as a moved endpoint. (2) The CSV of the FDA list of AI-enabled devices, unconditionally (141 KB; the limits, the host pin to `www.fda.gov` and the header check of `fda.ts`). (3) The composite validator, below. (4) The join: the K numbers of the list (`K` and six digits, as written) in batches of 50 and its PMA numbers (`P` and six digits as written, and a supplement `P…/S…` with the slash removed, since openFDA writes it without one) in batches of 30, as `search=<field>:(<n>+OR+<n>…)&limit=1000` (without `+OR+` the parentheses mean AND and nothing matches), each request 16 MiB, 60 s, to `api.fda.gov` only. More than 1,000 results for a batch are paged with `skip` (5 pages at most; more is `wrong_root`, and so is a page that is not the last and holds fewer than 1,000 results), and a batch whose answer is cut at the limit is split in half and asked again, down to 5 numbers. A 404 on the first page of a batch is no match for that batch, and a 404 on a later page fails the download as `wrong_root` (the search did not page as it was asked); any other failure fails the download, a 429 with its `Retry-After`. De Novo numbers (`DEN…`) are not searched, and the base number of a supplement is not searched either: a recalled product is joined to a row of the list only when one of its `k_numbers` or `pma_numbers` equals the row's term exactly, so a recall that cites only the base PMA number of an AI-listed supplement is not joined. The requests are 500 ms apart (about 120 a minute, half of openFDA's limit of 240), and a download makes at most 80 requests and takes at most 96 MiB of answers (more is `too_large`); the list of 2026-11-07 takes 36 before the class stage (the two probes, the CSV and 33 batches: 32 of K numbers and one of PMA numbers) and 43 in all. (5) The class stage: the distinct recall numbers of the joined products (`product_res_number`), 50 a request, each between quotes, as `search=recall_number:(…+OR+…)` on `device/enforcement.json`, one page of 1,000 at most. A recall has one report, so an answer of more than a page, or whose total is not the number of its results, is not the list (`wrong_root`); a 404 is "no report" for the batch, and an answer that is too large is split in half as in the join. Of a report the connector reads three fields and keeps two: the class, which must be exactly `Class I`, `Class II` or `Class III`, and the day it was classified (`center_classification_date`, eight digits that form a day). It never reads the status, because the FDA does not update it after the classification and openFDA says the data is no way to follow a recall. A report with another class gives no class and counts as a row that cannot be read; a report with a class whose day is missing or is no day keeps its class, has no day and counts as nothing, so that the record says that the classification day is not stated. Of two reports of one number the one kept is decided by their values and never by their place in the answer: the more serious class (a report with no class is the least), and of one class the earlier day (none is the latest). A number counts as one row that cannot be read only when none of its reports has a class. (6) Each answer is projected at once to the fields of the record, and the rest, `code_info` first, is dropped before the next request, so that what a bundle stores never holds it.
  - **No shortcut when nothing changed.** After the two probes and the CSV, the download builds a composite value, `recalls:<meta.last_updated>;enforcement:<meta.last_updated>;list:<the first 16 hexadecimal digits of the SHA-256 of the CSV text>`, and ends as `unchanged` when it equals the `lastModified` it is given, without the join or the class stage. The bundler gives no validators, so every build runs the whole download: about 43 requests and 18 MB of answers on 2026-11-07 (openFDA has no field projection and `code_info` can be megabytes), well within the 1,000 requests a day that openFDA allows one IP address.
  - **The record and what it leaves out.** One record per recall event (`res_event_number`, digits, 12 at most), whatever the number of products, since one firm can have dozens in one event. The text begins `FDA medical device recalls (openFDA), joined to the FDA list of AI-enabled medical devices.` and holds the event number, the recalling firms (3), the days initiated (the earliest of the products), posted (the earliest) and terminated (the latest, only when every product has ended), the status (the most open of the products), the root cause as the FDA records it (3 values at most), the reason for the recall (that of the first product, by recall number, that has one; 600 characters at most), the products (their number and 3 descriptions of 200 characters at most), the devices of the FDA list that the recall covers (3 at most, each with its submission number and company), the recall numbers (5 at most), the FDA's class of the event (the most serious class that any of its products has, Class I before II before III, how many of its products have it unless all do, and the earliest day the FDA classified any of those, or that the classification day is not stated when none of them has a day, from the enforcement reports; when no product has a class, the sentence that the FDA's recall page shows it), and the closing sentence that a recall is a firm's correction or removal of a device, classified by the FDA, not a finding that the device's AI caused the problem, and that openFDA says its results are unvalidated and not to be relied on for decisions about medical care. A cut ends in ` [shortened]`, and the summary is at most 4,000 characters. The recalling firm is not always the vendor (a subsidiary, a distributor), so the text names both the firm and the company of the FDA list, and both are name keys (weak, 50 at most for a record). The address is the FDA's recall page of the event. **Never stored:** `code_info`, the addresses of the firm, `additional_info_contact` (a named contact and a phone number), `distribution_pattern`, `product_quantity`, `action`, `firm_fei_number`, `cfres_id`, the `openfda` block (its GMDN content is licensed separately), the `k_numbers` and `pma_numbers` that were not joined, and every field of an enforcement report but its class and the day it was classified (the class is also an identifier of the record). `reason_for_recall` and `product_description` are free text that a model reads, so they are cleaned (control, zero-width and bidirectional characters out, white space collapsed), cut, and go through the same escaping and untrusted-data line as every source.
  - **Limits.** At most 5,000 events (more is `wrong_root`), 20 recall and submission numbers in the identifiers, and a recall number of the form `Z-NNNN-NN` (the years 2002 to 2006) or `Z-NNNN-NNNN`; a product with another number, or an initiation day that is not a day, is skipped and counted, with the tolerance of max(10, 1%) of the CVE records, and a report of the class stage whose class cannot be read is counted the same way, as one row and no more, since its product is one already (`skipped` counts the products left out, `classSkipped` the recall numbers none of whose reports has a class; a day that cannot be read counts as nothing). The shrink check applies to the events (155 in the live run of 2026-11-07).
  - **Coverage.** The category is AI-specific risks, which a run researches only for a vendor with a website: for a vendor with none the row is `not_researched`, and the list is on no "Checked" or "Not checked" line. A deployment has no copy until the load of its start has applied the list's bundle: until then the row is `failed` with no day of copy, under "Not checked".
- **The third round of lists** (Q35 to Q53, 2026-11-10; spec sections D2.21 to D2.43 and D4.14). On 2026-11-10 the product owner asked for every list of the research of 2026-11-09 that can be added at once, and delegated the decisions (see **The gates** above). Twenty-three lists were built, all open: 22 are copies and GLEIF is read through. The FDA device enforcement reports were folded into the FDA recalls (Q37, above), and eight lists of the research were not built (D2.44). The source registry stays at `sr-6`, since no host is added. `VRDB_SOURCES` holds the 23 after the FDA recalls in the order of the table above, and a run offers the records in that order: within a category the sanctions entries come before the enforcement entries and those before the product safety entries, and the lists that can raise a risk before the registers.
  - **The lists, by category.** Legal and regulatory matters (`legal_regulatory`, 10): Canada's sanctions list (`canada_sema`); the enforcement and case lists of the UK OFSI (`uk_ofsi`), the FTC (`ftc_penalties`, `ftc_competition`), the European Commission (`eu_dg_comp`), the UK CMA (`uk_cma`) and Colombia's SIC (`co_sic_penalties`); and the product safety lists of NHTSA (`nhtsa_recalls`, `nhtsa_odi`) and the UK MHRA (`uk_mhra`). Security incidents (`security_incidents`, 2): the California breach notices (`california_breaches`) and the disclose.io program list (`disclose_io`). AI-specific risks (`ai_specific`, 8): the AI incident lists (`charlotin`, `piperoll`) and six registers (`canada_ai_register`, `omb_ai_inventory`, `nl_algoritmeregister`, `uk_atrs`, `epoch_ai`, `fmti`). Certifications (`certifications`, 2): `fedramp` and `c2pa_conforming`. Financial health (`financial_health`, 1): `gleif`. Each is bundled with the others before a release and is stale 60 days after its bundle was built. A run researches certifications and AI-specific risks only for a vendor with a website, so for a vendor with none the lists of those two categories are `not_researched`.
  - **The list kit** (`vrdb/connectors/kit/`). The 22 copies of 2026-11-10 are specs of one factory (`createFileConnector` in `kit/fileConnector.ts`): where the files are, how to read them and what a row becomes, and the factory does the rest. It asks the files in order through the fetch it is given (`connectorFetch`, through the page guard), and the answer to each, a 304 included, must come from the pinned host and directory after the redirects (`kit/address.ts`), or the download fails as not allowed; a file cut at its limit fails as too large; and what the reader does not take for the list fails as not the list, as does any other error of a reader (no error holds an address, a row or a word of the file). A list of one file can be asked with validators and a list of several files is asked in full every time; the bundler sends no validators, so every build reads every file. The etag that the kit returns is `k<version>:<the server's etag>`, and validators are sent only when a given etag has the prefix of the version that reads now; a 304, or an `unchanged` of a spec's own fetch, when no validator was sent fails as unreachable. The connectors of 2026-11-10 started at `version` 1, since none of them had run against a stored snapshot before its first release; those whose reading changed what they store since (`charlotin`, `nl_algoritmeregister`, `uk_atrs`, `uk_cma`, `uk_mhra` and `uk_ofsi`, as `csl` of the round before) are at `version` 2 (a bundle built by a new version is not held to the shrink check against the previous bundle). A read that is partial is no read: a reader says whether its rows are the whole list (`complete`), and one that stopped at its limit of rows, at a quote that was never closed or at a row of too many columns fails the download as too large and never gives a shorter list, which would mark every record that it lacks as gone. The body is the rows sorted by the spec's key and then by their canonical text, so that the order of a file decides nothing. The records are built one row at a time by `recordOf` (`kit/record.ts`), the one place where a record is made: an id of more than 200 characters is no id, a title is cut at 200 characters and a summary at 1,300 (the cut says so, with ` [shortened]`), no `<` or `>` is left in a title, a summary, an identifier or a key (they are written as the angle quotation marks), the phrase "lookup note" is written "lookup-note" in a title and a summary, and a name, an LEI or a number is folded like a cell before it is a key. A text that must be a name, a key or an id is read with `lineWithin` (`kit/clean.ts`), which gives a clean line that fits or nothing and never a cut one, and the keys refuse any text that ends in the cut marker, so that no cut text is ever matched; the ids, identifiers and displays are each connector's to keep uncut. The rows that cannot be read, and the entries that the builder refuses, are counted against an allowance of ten or a hundredth of the rows when that is more, and never more than a tenth; beyond it the parse fails as a new format. A reader that groups raw rows into entities gives a `total` that is the larger of the raw rows and the entities plus the unreadable ones, and one that counts its unreadable ones in raw lines (the two NHTSA files) says so with `allowanceBasis`. The other files of the kit are `clean.ts` (lines, days, angle brackets), `table.ts`, `json.ts`, `text.ts` and `stream.ts` (the readers of hostile text, which read once from the front and stop at a limit), `archives.ts` (one file of a zip, one sheet of a workbook), `paging.ts` (the several requests of one list, each pinned), `summary.ts` (a few sentences and a closing sentence that is never cut), `persons.ts` and `pause.ts`.
  - **How a record begins.** The text of every record begins with the list's mark, the operator's own host in parentheses (never the host of a file, a CDN or a storage bucket: `nhtsa.gov`, `international.gc.ca`, `github.com/FedRAMP`, `gov.uk`) and the kind of list, as in `NHTSA defect investigations (nhtsa.gov), a product safety list.` The kinds are `a sanctions list`, `an enforcement or case list`, `a product safety list`, `an AI incident list`, `a data breach notice list` and `a register or status list`, and the analysis prompt names each list by its mark (`<ID>_RECORD_MARK`, exported by its connector). No mark is the prefix of another. Since `vr-16` the analysis reads a source as a record only by its `database` attribute, which code writes from the provenance of the record and whose value is the mark: a page that begins with a mark is an ordinary page.
  - **Events and states.** The 12 lists that record matters are events. Their record types are `breach_notice_summary`, `sanctions_penalty_notice`, `hallucination_cases`, `civil_penalty_summary`, `agent_incidents`, `eu_competition_cases`, `competition_case_summary`, `competition_action_summary`, `consumer_sanction_summary`, `vehicle_recall_summary`, `field_safety_notice_summary` and `defect_investigations`, all in `EVENT_RECORD_TYPES` of `vrdb/lookup.ts`: each is dated by its newest matter, the lookback of the category applies to that day (36 months for legal and regulatory matters, 24 months for security incidents and AI-specific risks), and the record is offered with it. A party of `uk_cma` with an open case has no day, and the lookback lets it through. The other 11 are states, offered as of the day of the copy, with no lookback: `sanctions_entity` (Canada's sanctions list, as for the other sanctions lists), `algorithm_register_supplier` (shared on purpose by `uk_atrs`, `canada_ai_register`, `omb_ai_inventory` and `nl_algoritmeregister`), `ai_model_developer`, `transparency_index_score`, `c2pa_conformance`, `cloud_authorization_status`, `disclosure_program` and `lei_record`. Every type is at most 30 characters, as `record_type` is `VARCHAR(30)` (the longest are `algorithm_register_supplier` and `field_safety_notice_summary`, 27).
  - **Weak lists, and lists that give context only.** A list that can raise a risk is matched by name only, so its record is weak and carries the lookup note: the label is `low` at most. For the AI incident, enforcement and case, product safety and breach lists, prompt `vr-15` lets an entry raise at most one risk, in the category of its list, worded "An entity named … appears in …" (for the California list, "An entity named … reported a data breach to the California Attorney General"), with a likelihood of Possible at most and an action plan that is a check of who the vendor is against the entry; Canada's sanctions list joins the sanctions paragraph of the prompt and is worded as the other sanctions lists. Eight lists were built weak, because their match is by name only and their identifiers are thin or a name is taken from a title: `uk_atrs`, `uk_cma`, `uk_ofsi`, `uk_mhra`, `piperoll`, `charlotin`, `nl_algoritmeregister` and `ftc_competition`. Ten lists give context only (`CONTEXT_ONLY_RECORD_SOURCES` in `records/types.ts`): `canada_ai_register`, `omb_ai_inventory`, `nl_algoritmeregister`, `uk_atrs`, `epoch_ai`, `fmti`, `c2pa_conforming`, `fedramp`, `disclose_io` and `gleif`; two of them, `uk_atrs` and `nl_algoritmeregister`, are also built weak, so only the other six built-weak lists raise a risk. The prompt tells the model that an entry of a register or status list raises no risk, whatever it says, that a vendor missing from one is no risk either, and that the entry may only be used as context in the description of another risk, worded as the register's own statement. The code backs that twice: the evidence pack gives such records up before any other source, and they never support a credibility label (see [Credibility](#credibility)). Context only is not name only: `fedramp` and `disclose_io` carry a domain key (strong against a verified domain), `uk_atrs` a Companies House number and `gleif` an LEI, and a strong record of such a list is still no support for a label.
  - **Natural persons.** A party that is a natural person is dropped before anything is stored, by rules that were each measured on their own list, with their word lists copied as they are and not merged (`connectors/kit/persons.ts`): the FTC penalties (`hasLegalForm`, `looksLikePerson`; its precision is low, so a firm whose name has the shape of a person's, two capitalized words and no legal form, is dropped as well), the EU competition cases (`euOrgEvidence`: a cartel case and a DMA case keep every name, and in any other antitrust case a name is kept only when it shows an organization or occurs in two cases or more, so that a person named in a cartel or DMA case, or in two cases, stays), the Colombian sanctions (`looksLikePersonEs`, asked of the whole cell and then of each side of it) and the California breach notices (`californiaPracticeRule`, a practice named for a person, and `CALIFORNIA_MIDDLE_INITIAL_NAME`; a few plain two-word names of persons are not caught, and the record of one is offered only to a vendor that has exactly that name). Every rule fails closed, a company being lost rather than a person kept, and a name that a rule drops is never an id, a key, a display, a title, a part of the text or an identifier, and is counted nowhere. The MITRE ATLAS connector shows the actor of a case study only when every part of it is a description from a closed set of words or an organization that is not person-shaped (`atlasActorOf`, built on the kit's word lists and `looksLikePerson`); a name, a handle or anything it is unsure of is left out, and the free prose of the description, which may credit a researcher, is a residual. Four rules stay in their connectors, because only one list needs each: the shape of a person's name in the OFSI notices, the honorifics of the CMA cases (Mr, Mrs, Ms and Miss, with or without a full stop: a party of that form is dropped, and its case stays for the other parties), the terms that make a C2PA applicant the business of a person and the academic titles of the Dutch register. What the main rules read, and what each leaves open, is described in the paragraphs that follow this one. Canada's sanctions list tells individuals from entities by its own elements (a record that has an element that only an individual or a ship has, such as a given name, a family name, a ship's number or a date of birth, is skipped before any other cell of it is read). Where the field that would name a person is never read, no rule is needed: the AI Hallucination Cases database reads free text in its tool cell only, every distinct value of which was read when the list was designed and none is a person, and the explanation of a vendor's dispute only for which tools it names, never keeping it, and never its case names, pointers, sources, details or items; PipeRoll takes no full name (an operator that is an individual is out by its category, the first party of a case caption, the text before the first `v`, `v.`, `vs` or `vs.` that has a blank on each side, in any letter case, is dropped, a name is one token with no blank, and a word that has the shape of a surname with a prefix or of an account handle is never a name; its residuals are below); the UK transparency records are read in their section of the supplier and their section of the Companies House number only, never in the named officials and contact data of the rest of the text (no person rule is applied to the supplier section, so a supplier named by a person's name, a sole trader, is kept as written); and the authors of Epoch AI's models, its Hugging Face developer id and the contact fields of the lists that have them are never read. Other lists apply no filter, each for a reason its connector states: the Government of Canada AI Register has no rule that tells a person from a company in its vendor cell (the best was one hit in five), so the residual under one percent is accepted; the filers and manufacturers of the two NHTSA files are businesses (every name of the real files was read when the lists were designed, and a rule by the shape of a name had no precision); the US federal AI use case inventory has no natural person among the strings of its vendor cells that were read when the list was designed, and none is kept apart; the MHRA lists name the manufacturers of medical devices, which are companies, and a rule of honorifics would only flag a company named after a person; the organization cell of Epoch AI's models names organizations (every token of the real file was read when the list was designed, and none was a person) and the column headers of the Foundation Model Transparency Index are the brand names of organizations; the FedRAMP file names companies, its only persons being the contacts of the assessors and the mailboxes, which are never read; the disclose.io list names organizations and products, its only individuals being customers' or users' own subdomains on hosting platforms, which sit in the two scope entries that are never read; and the FTC competition list has no field but a matter title, so it cuts the parts of a title where a natural person is named (every parenthetical group and everything after the first hyphen with a blank on each side) and applies no person rule, since none was found that works on a title, so that a person named in the party part of a title would be a party. A GLEIF record is the vendor's own, by its stored LEI, and holds no address, so no filter by the kind of entity is applied.
  - **Natural persons: the FTC penalties.** The names of the defendants are the one place where a person can be named. Every parenthetical group of a case name is cut before anything else is read (individual defendants and brands are written there), except a nickname, and the party rule is asked of what is left: a party that is a person's name is dropped, with its row's share of the case, before anything is stored. The kit's rule has a low precision, so a firm whose name has the shape of a person's is dropped as well.
