One pass starts at most `scanPassRuns` runs over all the organizations together: the runs that `VENDOR_RESEARCH_WORKER_CONCURRENCY` places can begin within the 15-minute start limit at about 7.5 minutes a run (10 with the default of 5), and never fewer than 5, so one organization scans as before. A pass that queued more left its last runs waiting past the start limit, to be swept as `timed out` and picked again the next hour, always for the organizations with the highest ids. The pass begins at another organization each hour (the hour since the epoch, modulo the number of organizations), so the ones that do not fit in a pass take turns. The job of a scheduled run also has a BullMQ priority (`SCHEDULED_RUN_PRIORITY`), and a job with none (a manual or automatic run) is always taken before the scheduled jobs that are still waiting, so a person's run waits at most for the scheduled runs already running to free a place.

An organization goes on its own: a failure in one is logged by the name of the error and the others are still scanned. Only the failure to find the organizations makes the job fail, and the next hour does the work again. The scan logs counts and ids only, and nothing when it started no run.

A scheduled run is a normal run with two differences: it analyses only the sources that are new (see below), and it tells the assignee of the vendor when it stored a finding (see [Notice to the assignee](#notice-to-the-assignee)). It counts toward the daily cap like every other run.

### The memory of the sources

A scheduled run researches the vendor in full, with the same loop, searches and reads, but does not ask the model again about what an earlier run already showed to it. Every completed run records the sources of its evidence pack in `vendor_research_sources`; a scheduled run sets apart the sources it finds again, and the pack, the analysis and the validation see only the new ones. A manual or an automatic run records its sources and sets nothing apart: a person who asks again wants everything looked at again. Every run reads the memory for its de-duplication all the same (see **When it is written** below).

- **What a source is.** The address alone, as `sourceKey` makes it (see the [table](#vendor-research-sources-table)): two addresses of one document (a tracking parameter, an AMP wrapper, `www.`) are one source. The first scheduled run of a vendor that has no memory analyses everything and starts it; vendors that were researched before the memory existed are not filled in afterwards.
- **When it is written.** After the transaction that stores the findings and ends the run commits, and only for a run that completed. A run that failed, or whose model call failed, remembers nothing, so the next scheduled run shows its sources to the model. (A scheduled run that failed after it started holds its vendor back for a day, so that next run is the next day's scan.) A source cited by a candidate that the caps cut (3 per category, 8 per run; `capped` in the result of `analyse`) is not recorded, even when a stored finding cites it too, so the next scheduled run shows it to the model again. The next run of any kind raises the cut risk, not only the next scheduled one. Every run reads the memory before its de-duplication (`loadKnown` in `pipeline.ts`): a scheduled run uses the memory it read to set sources apart, and a manual or automatic run reads it only for the de-duplication and still shows the model every source. A URL that a stored finding cites and no run remembers having shown (`forgotten`, passed to `dropDuplicates`) drops a candidate of that category only when the candidate is also at least 0.25 similar (Jaccard, `ACTIVE_RISK_THRESHOLD`) to that finding, so the cut risk is stored and a rewording of the stored finding is still dropped. A stored finding whose source the memory does not hold for another reason (a finding from before the memory, a memory write that failed) is treated the same way. It records the sources that the model was shown and the ones that were set apart, so `last_seen_at` moves. A page change is not remembered as a source: its document is its page, which a run remembers only when the model was shown the page itself, so a run whose pack held only the change does not set the page apart in later scheduled runs. A change that the model was shown is remembered under a key of its own instead: the key of its page followed by its fragment, `<page key>#changed-<8 hex>` (`changeKeyOf` in `sourceMemory.ts`). No key of a document has a fragment, so that key never sets a source apart in a scheduled run. The de-duplication looks a change up by its change key (`forgottenIn` in `sourceMemory.ts`), so a stored finding that cites a change that was shown keeps the `same_source` rule. A candidate that the caps cut takes the change key out of the memory with its other keys, so a change it cites lets it back in, as any other source it cites does. A memory that cannot be written is logged by the name of the error and changes nothing about the run; the next scheduled run then analyses those sources again.
- **When it cannot be read.** A scheduled run whose memory cannot be read goes on as a run that analyses everything. In a run of any kind whose memory cannot be read, the old rule holds in the de-duplication: a candidate that cites a URL a stored finding of its category cites is dropped as `same_source`. The warning in the log says what the run does without the memory, and names the error only by its name. A scheduled run logs `the sources of the earlier runs could not be read, so every source is analysed and a source that a stored finding cites drops a candidate of its category (error <name>)`. A manual or automatic run logs `the sources of the earlier runs could not be read, so a source that a stored finding cites drops a candidate of its category (error <name>)`.
- **What the summary says.** A category without a new source is `nothing_found` with the fixed note `No new sources since the last run`, which the screen shows as "No new sources were found since the last run." A run where nothing is new ends without a model call. The trace counts what was set apart as `dropped["source.already_seen"]`.
- **Not pruned.** A row is not deleted when a source stops appearing; deleting the vendor or the organization takes its rows with it.
- **Known limit: the memory only adds.** A source that an earlier run recorded stays recorded when a later run cuts a candidate citing it. A scheduled run then sets the source apart, and a run of any kind drops a candidate that shares that remembered source with a stored finding of its category as `same_source`, so a manual run raises the candidate only when no stored finding of its category cites the source.
- **Known limit: vendors researched before the memory.** The memory is not filled in for them, so their stored findings cite URLs that no run remembers. Until a run remembers those URLs, every run, not only a scheduled one, drops a reworded repeat of such a finding on a shared URL only at Jaccard 0.25 or above, and no longer on the shared URL alone. The same holds for a change of a page of the vendor's terms that a run showed before changes had keys of their own: the memory does not hold its change key, so a stored finding that cites it is treated as forgotten (the rule of Jaccard 0.25 above) unless a later run shows that same change again.
- **Known limit.** A page that changes at an address the memory knows is not analysed again by a scheduled run. The pages where the vendor states its terms are the exception: their changes are watched on their own (see [Policy page changes](#policy-page-changes)).
- **Known limit: a summary record that gains items.** The lists that summarise one entity (the California breach notices, the FTC and the Colorado penalties, the NHTSA recalls and crash reports, the UK CMA and MHRA lists, the FTC competition list) keep one address per entity (`?organisation=<id>`, `?filer=<id>`, and so on) while the text of the record gains a new item. The dedupe rule `same_source` drops a candidate that cites an address an earlier finding of the category cited, whatever its status (every run makes one exception, for an address that no run remembers having shown: see [The memory of the sources](#the-memory-of-the-sources)), and a scheduled run sets the record aside as already seen. So a new breach, penalty or recall of an entity that was flagged once is not raised again, and a finding the person dismissed as not their vendor does not come back either. That is one finding per entry, as the prompt asks. A per-version address would raise every entity already flagged once more and needs a transition rule in the dedupe and a decision of the owner on whether a dismissed entry may resurface; it is not built (audit of 2026-11-11, finding PL-1).
- **Known limit: RLS Phase 2 and the start of a run.** The tenant isolation policies on the vendor research tables are installed, and the app connects as the table owner today, so they have no effect yet. Under Phase 2 (the app role, `rls-rollout.md`) the start of a run takes its advisory lock on the transaction of the request when the request has one (`withRunStartLockQuery`), so the lock is held until the request commits, which includes the enqueue (at most 5 seconds). Two things stay for that rollout, both older than the audit: the job of a run is queued before the run row commits, so a worker can take a job whose run it cannot see yet (the run then stays queued until the sweep), and the automatic start after a vendor is created (`triggerAutoResearch` in `vendor.ctrl.ts`) is not awaited, so under Phase 2 its savepoint can race the request's own queries.

### Policy page changes

A vendor's terms, privacy policy, data processing addendum, list of subprocessors and security page change without a press release: a clause that lets it train models on customer data, a new subprocessor, a lost certification. The research reads these pages in most runs, and the memory of the sources would hide a page whose address it knows. So the pages where the vendor states its terms are watched on their own, in every kind of run, manual, automatic and scheduled alike: each completed run keeps their text in `vendor_research_snapshots`, and the next run compares what it reads with what was kept. A page that changed is a source of its own.

- **Which pages.** A page of the vendor's own site (its domain, a subdomain, or the domain its home page moved to; decided on the address that answered; a domain that is exactly a host where many have pages, such as `github.com` for a vendor whose website is `github.com/acme`, is none, as in the ledger of the research) that the session read, whether the model asked for it or the routine work did, and whose address or title says what it states: `subprocessors`, `dpa`, `privacy`, `terms` or `security` (`policyKindOf`; a blog post, a file, a page about pricing and the home page are none of them). `collectPolicyPages` wraps the reader that the web session gets and notes the page as it is read, with its whole text and not the excerpt that the ledger keeps. It never stops a read. At most 8 documents for a run; a document read twice counts once, with the text of the later read, and a page with no text never takes the place of one that had some. The check of the website, the sitemap and the reads that check the findings go around the session and note nothing.
- **First visit.** A page that the vendor has no kept text for is only kept: there is nothing to compare it with. Nothing is filled in afterwards for vendors that were researched before this existed.
- **The comparison** (`comparePages`, before the evidence pack is built). The lines of the two versions are compared as sets and without regard to case: a paragraph that moved is no change, and neither is a line that only says when the page was written (`Last updated ...`, `Effective ...`, `© 2026 ...`). A change of fewer than 40 characters in all is minor: no source is made, and the new text is still kept, so it is not compared again. A line longer than 600 characters (`MAX_LINE_CHARS`) is compared as its parts (see the table `vendor_research_snapshots` above), so a change anywhere in a long clause is a change: its part is compared, and shown to the model (on a page that the limits cut, only inside the kept window, below). A kept line that is still on the page is never reported as removed, even when the limits left it out of the new text. When the kept version stopped at the limits (or may have: `mayHaveBeenCut`), a new line of the page is reported as added only when it comes before the end of the kept window (`keptWindowLength` in `policySnapshots.ts`), since a line after it may have been on the page past the old limit. The window is placed by a rule that states only what it can prove. It ends at the second line of the last anchor pair. An anchor pair is two lines of the page (the whole page as it reads now, past the limits too), one right after the other, that the kept body and the page each hold exactly once, compared without regard to case. They follow each other in the kept body too, in the same order. The second begins a line of the page and is not a later part of the same long clause, since two parts of one line are one line of the old page and a copy of that line would bring both. The first comes later in the kept body than every line before it on the page that the kept body and the page each hold once, so a pair that moved up, or copies that stand out of order, never end the window. With no such pair the window is empty and nothing is reported as added. That holds also for a page that holds none of the kept lines: it is not taken as new as a whole, since the kept lines may have been deleted and the page be old text that stood past the cut. Why a line before the pair is new: an edit that inserts, deletes or replaces lines keeps the old lines it leaves in their old order, and every line of the old page before the pair is in the kept body, which is the start of the old page. So when the pair on the page is the kept copy, a line before it that the kept body lacks is new (when the pair is not the kept copy, see the known limit of the kept window below). One line that both hold once is not enough to end the window: a kept line deleted whose copy stands past the cut would make the old text before that copy read as added. Lines that the kept body or the page repeat (table cells, `Back to top`) are never part of a pair, since the copy they stand for is not known. The window does not touch what is removed: every kept line that the page, past the limits too, no longer holds is removed. A page that did not change holds the kept lines in order, and nothing is reported. A line inserted or replaced before a pair is reported, so a change of lines inside the window and a line inserted before the last two kept lines are shown. What is not shown is under the known limit of the kept window below. One pass over the kept lines and two over the page, so the time is linear. A change past the 100,000-character limit of the new text is not seen. A text kept before long lines were split is compared as it was made until a run keeps the page in the new form (see the table above).
- **The source.** A change is a ledger entry like the pages that were read: kind `page`, on the vendor's site, titled `Change to <title of the page>`, dated the day of the run. Its text begins with `Page change:`, says which page changed between which days (the day the page was last seen as it was and the day of the run), and lists the `Added:` and the `Removed:` lines, at most 6,000 characters in all, a line at most 600, the added lines at most 60% of the room when lines were removed too, and the number of lines that did not fit. Each line is a bullet (`- `), except a part of a long clause that continues the line shown right before it: that one is written with two spaces (`CONTINUATION_INDENT`) in place of the bullet and the continued mark, so that, once white space is squeezed, the two lines read as the stretch of the page they are, with one space between them (`takeLines`). A part continues the line before it only when all of these hold (`joinsAtBlank` and `linesNotIn`): it is a part after the first (it begins with `… `), it is the line right after that one on the page (both are in the list, with no line of the page between them), and the part before it was cut where a blank was. The last means that the text of the part before (without its mark) is longer than half its room and shorter than its room less one, or exactly its room less one with a blank in its second half; the room is 600 characters for a first part and 598 for a later one. A part cut at the limit in the middle of a word (a long URL, text with no blanks such as Chinese or Japanese) may or may not have had a blank at the cut, so it is never joined, and nor is a part whose part before is not shown or does not fit: it keeps `- … `. An indented line costs the same room as a bullet. The data that is stored and hashed does not change: the joints are worked out when the change is described. Its category is `certifications` for the security page and `ai_specific` for the others. Its address is the page's own with a fragment, `#changed-` and the first 8 characters of the hash of the new text, so each change has an address of its own: the same change is the same address (and the de-duplication knows a finding that cited it), and a later change of the page is another. The sources come first in the ledger, so they come first in their category and the evidence pack trims them last. In a scheduled run they are set beside the memory of the sources and not under it: a page whose address the memory knows still shows its change, and a category with such a source counts as having a new source.
- **The analysis.** The system prompt tells the model what a source with `change="terms"` is. Since `vr-16` code writes that attribute from the provenance of the entry (`provenanceOf` in `evidence.ts` marks an entry on the vendor's own site, with no record, an address that ends in the `#changed-` fragment and a text that begins with `Page change:`), and a page of a third party that begins with `Page change` gets none and is an ordinary page. Nor does a change of a stranger's page on the host where many have pages (the hosts of `sharedHosts.ts`, the code, model, package, document and app hosts among them) that a vendor's website is a page of: no page of that host is the vendor's own, so none is noted or compared. The rule: raise a risk only when a changed line is itself an adverse term, a new subprocessor or AI provider, a weaker commitment or a lost certification; rewording, a new date or a change of layout is not a risk; quote a line without its `Added:` or `Removed:` mark and without its dash; a line that begins with two spaces and no dash continues the line before it on the page, the two are one clause with one space between them, and a quote may run across them; a line that begins with `…` is a later part of a long clause: quote it on its own, never with the line before it (since `vr-19`; `vr-18` said only that such a line continues the line before it; `CONTINUED_MARK` of `policySnapshots.ts`). Like every page of the vendor's site, it supports `medium` confidence on its own.
- **When it is written.** After the transaction that stores the findings commits, like the memory of the sources, and only for a run that completed: a run that failed keeps nothing, so a change that was not shown is shown by the next run. Every page that had text is written, also the ones that did not change (that moves `last_seen_at`), except a page whose change the model was not shown whole (`snapshotsToKeep` in `policySnapshots.ts`): the evidence pack drops sources to stay under its limit, changes too, and a change can be the source of a candidate that the caps cut. Such a page keeps its earlier text, so the next run compares with that text and shows the change again, dated from the day the page was last kept. Kept pages that cannot be read leave the run as one that read none of those pages and keep nothing; a write that fails is logged by the name of the error; neither changes the findings of the run.
- **In the trace.** `dropped["page.first_visit"]`, `dropped["page.unchanged"]` and `dropped["page.minor"]` count the pages that made no source, and `dropped["page.change_not_shown"]` counts the pages that were not kept because the pack dropped their change. The log line has counts only, never an address, a title or a line of a page.
- **Known limit: the first run after the change of the kept format.** A text kept before long lines were split (its hash is the plain SHA-256 of its body) is compared as it was made: the new text is cut at 600 characters a line. When what it holds is unchanged, the page counts as a minor change and is kept again in the new form. When the comparison finds a real change and the model is not shown that change whole, the page is not kept (`snapshotsToKeep`), and the next run compares the old text as it was made again. So a change after character 600 of a line is not seen until the first run that keeps the page in the new form, which need not be the next run. Such a text says in no hash whether the limits cut it, so `mayHaveBeenCut` (`policySnapshots.ts`) counts as the old code did: the text is taken as cut when its stored body (its length + 1) could not have held one more line of 600 characters under the 100,000-character limit, or when it has 4,000 lines. In such a run only the lines of the kept window are looked at for added lines, as above.
- **Known limit: the kept window.** The window never shows old text past the cut as added, except in the three cases below, and it pays for that with new lines that it does not show. Not shown is a new line after the last anchor pair. That covers a change in the clause the cut ends in, for a text kept now: its kept parts are parts of one line, so no pair follows the change (a text kept before long lines were split cut that clause to one line, so the heading and the clause after it make a pair, and the new wording is shown). It covers the last kept line replaced, or the kept line before it, on a page cut by either limit; a line inserted right before the last kept line, which reads as the last kept line moved down past an old line; a change near the end of a page whose last kept lines repeat (such as `Back to top`); any change on a page that is one long paragraph, since its parts never make a pair; and any change on a cut page whose lines held once are never neighbours, such as a table with one cell a line and repeated cells. Only the old wording is shown, under `Removed:`, and when what was removed is under 40 characters (`MIN_CHANGED_CHARS`) no entry is made at all. Either way the page is kept with its new text, so the new wording becomes the text that the next run compares with, without ever having been shown (the test `keeps the page once its change was shown, though the new wording was not` pins this). Old text reads as added in three cases. First, lines moved or reordered: a line from past the cut moved into the window, or neighbouring kept lines moved down past the cut together, whose old lines before them then read as added. Second, the old page held two neighbouring kept lines again past the cut and the vendor deleted the kept copies: the stored row and the page are the same as for those lines moved down, so no rule that reads only the stored text and the new page can tell the two apart (the test `cannot tell kept lines deleted, with copies past the cut, from the lines moved down` pins it). Third, a line inserted before the pair whose text also stood past the cut on the old page: it is a copy, and it reads as added (the test `shows as added a copy of a line past the cut that is inserted before the last pair` pins it, and its neighbour `shows as added a new line inserted before the last pair` pins a new line there). In `policySnapshots.window.test.ts` the tests `cannot tell a line inserted before the last kept line from the last kept line moved down`, `cannot tell the last kept line replaced from it deleted, on a page cut by the limit of lines` and `cannot tell the line before a repeated last kept line replaced from the last two deleted` pin lines that are not shown (the second under `a page cut by the limit of lines`, a page that the limit of lines really cuts, whose short lines make no entry; its neighbour `shows what a long last kept line was, not the line that replaced it` pins the `Removed:` entry of a long one), and the tests under `old text past the cut is never shown as added (RV8P-1..4)` pin the deletions across the cut, the repeated lines and the copies past the cut that show no old text, and, under `copies past the cut of kept lines whose kept copies are deleted`, the second and the third case, which do. A recommended follow-up, not done here since it is a decision about what is stored: keep with the snapshot a fingerprint of the first line that the limits cut off. The end of the kept window could then be proved, and a change at the end of a cut page shown.
- **Known limit: the joints of a change.** The kept lines cannot tell a part from a line of the page that itself begins with `… `: such a line, right after a line as long as a part, is shown as continuing it. A part with no letter or digit is dropped from the kept lines (`squeezedLines`), so the two parts around it count as next to each other and may be joined across it. A quote across a cut made where no blank was is refused (see [Validation](#validation)).
- **Known limits.** Only the pages that a run happens to read are compared, and a page that cannot be read (robots.txt refuses it, or a script builds it and the headless browser is off) is not seen in that run. The text is kept as the page reader gave it, the navigation and the footer of the site included, which is why the lines are compared as sets and the stamps are left out.

### Live progress

While a run works, the page shows what it is doing. The pipeline writes a `RunProgress` (see the column above) to the run: first `researching` with no turn taken, when the models are resolved and just before the research starts; then after every turn of the research loop (the researcher is given a `progress` callback, and its observer reports the turn, with the counts of the web session at that moment); and `analysing`, with the counts the research ended with, when the analysis is about to start. A run with nothing usable to analyse never reports `analysing`.

`updateRunProgressQuery` writes it, and only to a run that is `running`, in the organization it is given: a write that comes late, after the run ended, finds no row, so it cannot bring a finished run back to life, and the queries that end a run empty the progress in the same statement. The writes never fail or slow a run: a write is sent at once when none is on its way, a report that comes while one is on its way waits and replaces any older one that was waiting (so the page always gets the latest state, never an older one after a newer one, and a slow database cannot build up a backlog), and a write that fails is logged by the name of its error and nothing else.

### Category outcomes

Each category ends a run with one of five outcomes and a note. "Worked on" means that at least one call tagged with the category reached the web; "answered" means that the web answered it: a search that returned an answer (an empty one too) or a page that was read. A call that the rules refuse before it reaches the web (a spent budget, an address that is not allowed, an invalid step) is not work on a category, and neither is a call that the loop's time limit cut off: it keeps its unit of budget but is not recorded. "Looked and found nothing" is thereby never confused with "could not look" or "did not look". The first rule that applies decides:

| Outcome | When | Stored note |
|---------|------|-------------|
| `skipped` | The category needs the vendor's domain and the vendor has none | `Vendor website is missing or not a valid domain` |
| `findings` | At least one finding was kept for the category, also when some of its calls failed | `1 potential risk found` or `N potential risks found` |
| `search_failed` | No call was recorded for the category, and a search of it failed because the engine did not answer while none was answered (`searchUnavailable` of the research, read from the run's search turns: a search that the clock of the routine work cut off while the engine had not answered leaves no call) | `The search was not available` |
| `skipped` | No call was recorded for the category: the time or the steps ran out, or the research ended, before it was covered (a call cut off by the time limit is not recorded, so its category reads `Not researched: the time limit was reached`) | `Not researched: the time limit was reached` (the loop ran out of time); `Not researched: the research ended before this category was covered` (any other end) |
| `search_failed` | The category was worked on and no call was answered; or it is a news category (not `certifications` or `ai_specific`) whose searches were none of them answered (it made some, or `searchUnavailable` names it), whatever pages of it were read, unless the next row applies (this comes before the rows below, and a kept finding still wins) | `The search was not available` (every call was a failed search, or the news category's searches all failed); `The pages could not be read` (otherwise) |
| `nothing_found` | A news category of the row above that read a page, and every source of which is the vendor's own page or a record | `News search was not available; checked on the vendor's own pages only` |
| `over_cap` | At least one call was answered, no finding of the category stayed, and the caps of the run cut a risk of it that held up (this comes before the next row; a kept finding still wins) | `More risks were found than one run keeps` |
| `nothing_found` | At least one call was answered and no finding survived | `Possible risks were found but could not be verified or are already recorded` (the model proposed a risk and none stayed); `No risks found; N of M pages could not be read` (a page call of the category failed); `No risks found; N of M searches were not available` (a search of the category failed and another call was answered); `No risks found; N of M searches were not available and P of Q pages could not be read` (both); `No new sources since the last run` (a scheduled run, when none of the sources of the category is new; a failure counted above comes first); otherwise the model's own note, which the UI shows as unverified, or `No risks found in the sources reviewed` when it wrote none |

A run stored before `over_cap` existed kept such a category as `nothing_found` with the note `More risks were found than one run keeps`. The UI maps each phrase to a translated sentence and shows nothing it does not know. The enum `search_failed` is labelled "could not be researched" in the UI. When a news category reads `The search was not available` (on `search_failed`) or `News search was not available; checked on the vendor's own pages only`, the run summary also says, without a click, "News search was not available, so results may be incomplete." (`newsSearchUnavailable` in `vendorResearchText.ts`).

### Model call

The analysis (`analyse` in `analysis.ts`, which also runs the validation and the de-duplication below, so that the pipeline and the [evaluation](../infrastructure/web-research.md#evaluating-the-agent) run the same code) is one call to `generateObjectWithSelfCorrection` (`advisor/llmSelfCorrect.ts`), with the model of the analysis key (the key of the research unless an Admin chose another; see [Which key a run uses](#which-key-a-run-uses)), temperature 0, under one 120 second deadline. It has no actions: it reads the evidence pack and nothing else. The answer must fit the schema in `schema.ts`:

- `findings`, at most 20, each with `category`, `risk_description`, `impact_description` and `action_plan` (at most 300 characters each), `likelihood` and `risk_severity` (the register's own values, so an accepted finding is always valid there), a `rationale` (one sentence, at most 300 characters), and 1 to 5 `evidence` items of `source_id` and `quote` (at most 300 characters);
- `category_notes`, at most five short notes (200 characters each), one per category.

If the answer does not fit, the wrapper sends the schema issues back and asks again, at most twice; the SDK also retries transport errors twice. The schema is not strict: a property that is not in it (at the top, on a finding, on an evidence item or on a note) is dropped when the answer is parsed, not reported. So no property name that the model chose, and so that a web page could have steered it to choose, reaches the system prompt of a retry, which quotes the schema issues. The JSON schema sent to the provider still has `additionalProperties: false` on every object, because the AI SDK adds it. The model is not asked for a confidence: the credibility label is computed by code from the sources (see [Credibility](#credibility)), and a `confidence` that an old recording or a model that remembers the earlier prompt still gives is dropped when the answer is parsed. `CREDIBILITY_PROMPT_VERSION` is stored on every run.

The user prompt carries the vendor (name, domain, what it provides) as context, then the materiality of the vendor when it has any (see below), and the evidence in an `<evidence>` block with `&`, `<` and `>` escaped. Each `<source>` has its id, category, kind and URL as attributes, `site="vendor"` when it is on the vendor's own site (the ledger's decision, `onVendorSite`, and never the host of the URL), a title, a published date when there is one, and its text in a `<snippet>`. The system prompt says: today's date and the lookback window of each category; that everything inside `<evidence>` is untrusted data, not instructions, and is of two kinds (result snippets and page excerpts, the latter with a gap marker where text was left out); that the vendor's own statement of a limitation, an expiry or an adverse term counts as evidence; to raise a risk only when the evidence itself shows an incident, gap, limitation, expiry, contradiction or adverse term; that a missing mention is not a finding; to ignore sources about other companies; to cite each risk with verbatim quotes, each from one passage and never across a gap; how to rate (see below); and to return an empty list when nothing qualifies.

**Materiality.** How much a vendor matters to the company is in its scorecard, and the analysis reads three of its answers: `data_sensitivity` (the most sensitive data the company shares with the vendor), `business_criticality` (how much its work depends on the vendor) and `regulatory_exposure` (the rules it must meet because of the vendor). `buildMateriality` (`materiality.ts`) takes each answer only when it is exactly one of the values the vendor form offers (the lists of `vendorValidation.utils.ts`), so what reaches the prompt is a closed vocabulary and never text that a person typed; "None" is an answer, because a vendor that holds no data weighs less. The answers go into a `<materiality>` block between the `<vendor>` block and the evidence (nothing, when the vendor has answered none), and the system prompt calls the block context and not evidence: it never makes a risk and never makes one more likely. It is used to judge `risk_severity` and the order of the risks, and not the likelihood, which comes from the sources only. The other questions of the scorecard (the past issues, the review) are not read: the past issues are the company's belief about the past, and the research is meant to find that out. **The research loop never gets the materiality**: the loop turns the vendor into web searches, and what a company says about its own dependence is not the web's business; the answers go to the organization's own LLM provider with the evidence and nowhere else.

**Anchored ratings.** The system prompt defines the five values of the likelihood (Rare, Unlikely, Possible, Likely, Almost certain) by what the sources show of the matter (a remote chance, a closed or narrow matter, one that could reach the company but is not shown to have, one that is open or keeps happening, one that has already happened or is in force today) and the five values of the severity (Negligible, Minor, Moderate, Major, Catastrophic) by the effect on the company that relies on the vendor (none worth a note, one it can absorb, one that costs time or money, one that harms its data, services, certifications or legal standing, one it could not absorb). It tells the model to rate what the sources show for this vendor and not the worst case of its kind, and that the two ratings are independent: a likely event can have a small effect, and a rare one a large effect. The rationale is the model's sentence on why the two ratings are what they are; it is written from the sources, shown to the person who decides, and never used by the code to decide anything. The wording is checked by `prompts.test.ts` (every value has a definition of its own, in the order of the scale), and the numbers it states are those of the schema.

### Validation

`validateFindings` (`validation.ts`) trusts nothing the model says and uses no model.

- **Verbatim quote.** Every citation is checked on its own and removed unless its source id is in the evidence pack and its quote is a substring of that source's text (the snippet of a result or the excerpt of a page), after the prompt escaping is decoded and case, whitespace and quotation marks are ignored. A quote that holds the gap marker `[…]` of a page excerpt is removed too: it would join passages that are not next to each other on the page. A source cited twice counts once. The check joins nothing: every quote must be a verbatim stretch of its source. The source that a change of a page of the vendor's terms makes has no exception. A quote may run across two parts of a long clause there only because the change already writes a part that continues the line before it as an indented line (see [Policy page changes](#policy-page-changes)): once white space is squeezed, the bullet and the indented line are the clause as the page has it, with one space at the cut. A quote that runs, without the dash, across two bullets or across a `- … ` line and the line before it, or across a cut made in the middle of a word (no blank at the cut: Chinese or Japanese text, a long URL) is refused, whether it is written as the page has it or with a space added at the cut. Such a part can still be quoted on its own.
- **The source is about the vendor.** A citation is also removed unless its source mentions the vendor: it is the vendor's own page (the ledger's `onVendorSite` flag, for a vendor that has a domain; the host of the URL is not looked at), or its title or text names the vendor as a whole word (also without the legal form of the record: "Acme Analytics, Inc." is found as "Acme Analytics") or mentions the domain, or carries the LEI that the vendor's record holds as a whole code (`carriesStoredLei`: a sanctions entry that the LEI found under a former or a holding name shows its identification numbers in its text, so its citation stands; a registration number does not count, as it is unique only in its register). The name check is the matcher of `identity.ts`, the one the search uses to keep results that are not about the vendor out of the evidence. So a page asked for on the vendor's domain that redirected off it does not count as the vendor's own, and has to name the vendor like any other page. A page about a company with a similar name cannot get in.
- **The quote says more than the name.** A citation is also removed unless its quote has at least one word that is no part of the vendor's own words. A word is a run of letters and digits in any script, compared after the quote is normalised as for the verbatim check. The vendor's own words are those of its name, of its other names (see [Other names](#other-names) above: a quote that is only "Facebook" says no more for Meta Platforms than "Meta Platforms" does, and the vendor-mention check accepts either) and of its domain; `www`, `http` and `https` (a page writes the domain with them, and the vendor record does not keep them); and the possessive of each word of the names and of the domain, which is the word followed by `s`, because the apostrophe is removed before words are compared ("Acme's" is "acmes"). For the vendor Acme Analytics (`acme-analytics.com`), the quotes "Acme", "Acme Analytics", "acme-analytics.com", "www.acme-analytics.com", "https://acme-analytics.com" and "Acme's" do not count, while "Acme confirmed the incident", "SOC 2 Type II" and "ISO 27001" do. This is not a minimum length. A refused quote does not use up its source: a later quote of the same source can still count. So a second hostname whose only quote is the vendor's name or domain no longer lifts a finding to `high`. There is no list of words, so any quote with one word beyond these still counts, and this is not a closed list of exceptions: a company suffix ("Acme Analytics Inc"), a path after the domain ("https://acme-analytics.com/trust") and, for a vendor with no domain on record, the top-level domain ("acme-analytics.com") all still count.
- **Candidates.** A candidate is dropped when no citation is left, when its category has no source in the evidence pack, when a text is empty or longer than 300 characters, when an enum value is not valid, or when `calculateRiskLevel(likelihood, risk_severity)` gives no level. The stored `risk_level` is that level, spelled as the register spells it ("High risk").
- **No confidence.** The validation sets no ceiling and stores no confidence: the label of a kept finding is computed afterwards, from the sources it cites, by `credibilityOf` (see [Credibility](#credibility)). The confidence ceiling that the earlier rules set from the tier of the cited hosts (`sourceTiers.ts`) is gone with the flag; a publisher is now read from the registry (`sourceRegistry/publisher.ts`: the name of the site without its top-level domain, so one story on `bbc.co.uk` and `bbc.com` is one publisher, and an aggregator is no publisher at all).
- **Clean text.** The texts the model wrote (the three finding texts, the rationale, the quotes and the category notes) are cleaned like the evidence is: control characters other than tab, line feed and carriage return are removed and lone surrogates are replaced. PostgreSQL refuses them, and one would fail the save of the whole run.

Candidates are ordered most severe first. What the checks cannot tell is a page that says something false about the vendor, or a quote from a page that gives orders to a model: both are real text of a page that names the vendor. The person who accepts a finding is the check for that.

### De-duplication

The register and the list should not collect the same risk again, and a dismissed finding should not come back on the next run. `dropDuplicates` (`dedupe.ts`) compares the words of the description and the impact together, using the Jaccard score of `services/vendors/textSimilarity.ts` (lower case, letters and digits, words of three characters or more):

| A candidate is compared with | It is dropped when |
|------------------------------|--------------------|
| The vendor's active register risks | The score is 0.25 or more |
| The vendor's earlier findings, whatever their status | The score is 0.4 or more, or it is in the same category and cites a URL that finding cited (a URL that no run remembers having shown counts only with a score of 0.25 or more; when the memory cannot be read, every shared URL counts) |
| A candidate of this run that was kept | The score is 0.4 or more (the first is kept) |
| The events of this run | Every source it cites is in an event that a kept candidate cites (see below) |

**One event is at most one finding.** The research finds sources one by one, and a model can turn one attack into three findings (what happened, what may have been taken, who was told), each in its own words. `clusterSources` (`clusters.ts`) finds the sources of the evidence pack that report the same event, with no model: two sources are one event when both have a date, the later is at most 21 days after the earliest of the event (counted from the first, so a run of weekly stories does not chain into one), and they are of one kind of event (an intrusion, an outage, a lawsuit or an enforcement, read from the title and the first 300 characters of the text; a page of the vendor's own site is read by its title alone), or when they name the same vulnerability (a `CVE-` id). A source with no date joins an event only through a CVE id. The analysis shows the model the sources of an event together, in an `<event id="E1">` element, and asks for the event to be one finding; the code then keeps the first, that is the most severe, candidate of an event and drops a later one that cites only sources of events a kept candidate cites (`same_event` in the trace as `duplicate.same_event`), also when it is in another category. A candidate that cites a source outside every event, or an event nobody cited, stays. Two incidents of one kind in the same three weeks are taken for one; that is the cost of a rule with no model.

`duplicates_dropped` on the run counts them. The caps (3 per category, 8 per run) are applied after the de-duplication, in severity order, so risks that are already known cannot take the places of new ones on a second run. The next run of any kind raises them too: the sources of the candidates that the caps cut are not remembered, and a candidate that shares such a source with a stored finding is dropped only when it is at least 0.25 similar to that finding (see [The memory of the sources](#the-memory-of-the-sources)). Only a run whose memory cannot be read still drops such a candidate as `same_source`.

### The check of a finding that rests on snippets

A snippet is what a search engine shows of a page, cut, joined or reworded by the engine. The validation proves that the model copied the snippet right, not that the page says it. So once the caps have left the findings of a run (at most eight), `verifySnippetFindings` (`verifySnippets.ts`, called by `analyse` with the page reader of the research run) checks each finding whose cited sources are all search results, none of them a page that the research read:

1. It reads the page of the most credible cited source (by its registry category: official, trusted press, the vendor, unverified; of equals, the one the model cited first), and when that page cannot be read, the next one; two pages at most for a finding, one deadline of 20 seconds for all the reads, `robots.txt` and the guarded fetch as for every page the agent reads. A page that answered from another site than the one cited (a redirect off the host, such as an agency exit link or a moved article) is not taken as the cited page: it neither confirms nor contradicts the finding, the trace records the read as `failed` with the reason `redirected_off_site`, and the next source is tried (`answeredOnCitedSite` in `verifySnippets.ts`). For a vendor with a domain, a page that answered from the vendor's own site (that domain or a host under it) is never the cited site of another publisher, whatever the hosts and the groups below say: its text is the vendor's word and does not earn the label of an official body or of the press, so the read is `redirected_off_site` like any other. Only a cited address on the vendor's site too may answer there. That also holds for a cited host above the vendor's (`acme.com` cited, the vendor's `trust.acme.com` answered). `answeredOnCitedSite` can also take other domains of the vendor (`ownDomains`), but a run passes only the vendor's domain today, so a redirect to an alias domain of the vendor is judged by the other rules. Otherwise a redirect within the cited host, or between it and a host above or below it (`www.`), is the same site. So is another domain of the same organisation, in a short list written by hand (`SAME_SITE_GROUPS` in `verifySnippets.ts`): `bbc.com` and `bbc.co.uk` (the BBC publishes on both), `abcnews.go.com` and `abcnews.com` (ABC News moved to the second, and the old host redirects there; other sites under `go.com` are not ABC News, so only that host is listed), and `canada.ca` and `gc.ca` (the Government of Canada moved its sites from `gc.ca` to `canada.ca`, with redirects, and holds both domains alone). A host is in a group when it is one of its domains or under one (`tbs-sct.gc.ca` and `tbs-sct.canada.ca`, or `ic.gc.ca` and `www.canada.ca`, are the same site, in both directions). A group never applies when either host is on the vendor's site (`onDomainsOfOneOrganisation`). The registry must also give both addresses the same category, an official body or the trusted press, and neither may be a copy (`syndicated`), so the label of the cited address is the label of the page read. The list is not drawn from the source registry, because one entry there can hold several bodies of a kind (the data protection authorities: `datatilsynet.no` is Norway's and `datatilsynet.dk` Denmark's, and `aepd.es` and `dataprotection.ie` are two sites too) or every government under a suffix (`gov.de` beside `gc.ca`). Every other redirect off the host stays `redirected_off_site`: between the bodies of such an entry, between governments, an exit link, and any other site. Politico (`politico.com` and `politico.eu`) is not in the list, although one entry of the registry holds both hosts: a redirect between the two is `redirected_off_site`. That is a conservative choice, since only groups known to be one body are listed; adding a group is one line in `SAME_SITE_GROUPS` and its tests. A search result is classified by its cited address, so text another host served never earns the cited host's label.
2. A page that holds the quote (compared like the validation does; the parts between the gap marks of an engine, `…` or `...`, each count) **confirms** the finding. It is stored as it was.
3. A page that does not hold the quote marks the finding `snippet_only`, and the credibility rule calls that source contradicted, so the finding is labelled `low`. The finding is kept.
4. When no page can be read the finding is marked `snippet_only` and its label is capped at `medium`: nothing was found against it, but nobody has seen the page either.

A cited source that is a Brave search result keeps its quote only in case 2, with the page's own title and date; in cases 3 and 4 it is stored by address only, with `quote_withheld: true` (see [Brave text is never stored](#brave-text-is-never-stored)). The check reads the page's title and date for that (`ConfirmedPage`).

The list of findings shows a marked finding with the caution "Based on search results only: it could not be confirmed on the source pages." on its row, without a click (it is left out when the sentence of the credibility label already says so, see [Vendor research](#vendor-research)). The reads are in the [trace](../infrastructure/web-research.md#the-run-trace) of the run (phase `verification`, steps `by: "verification"` with the outcome `confirmed`, `not_on_page` or `failed`). The evaluation measures it as `snippetOnlyFindings`, looking the quotes up in the pages that the run read.

### The verifier

The verifier was told about databases in `vr-12`: on 2026-11-01 the first golden attempt saw it reject all five claims that rested on entries of the CISA catalog, because one quote of an entry cannot hold both the name of the catalog and the description of the flaw, and the verifier saw neither the database nor the whole record.

The checks above are made by code. What code cannot read is meaning: a source can name the vendor and be about another company with a similar name, a quote can be on the page and say less than the claim states, and a matter can be older than the lookback of its category when the source has no date to show it. So once the caps have left the findings of a run (at most eight), `verifierPasses` (`verifier.ts`, called by `analyse`) makes **one** more model call about all of them, with the model of the analysis and at the same time as the page reads of the check above.

- **What it reads.** The system prompt (`buildVerifierSystemPrompt`) names today's date and the lookback of every category; the user prompt (`buildVerifierUserPrompt`) holds the vendor and, for each finding, its claim, its impact and, for each source it cites, the address, the date, the title, the quote and, for an entry of a public database that code looked up for the vendor, the name of that database (the `database` attribute: "CISA Known Exploited Vulnerabilities Catalog", "National Vulnerability Database", "CVE Program", "Have I Been Pwned", "SEC EDGAR", "the vendor's status page" or the name of a list of the vendor risk database, a sanctions list, an AI list or one of the 23 lists of 2026-11-10 among them; `VERIFIER_DATABASE_NAMES` in `prompts.ts`, 39 names in all). It holds no other web text. For every finding the model answers three questions with true or false: `same_company` (are the sources about the vendor and not about another company with a similar name?), `quote_supports_claim` (does the quote, read on its own, say what the claim says? A source with a database is an entry of that public database, and that it is an entry of that database is part of what it says: an entry of the CISA Known Exploited Vulnerabilities Catalog says that the flaw is exploited in the wild, while an entry of the National Vulnerability Database or of the CVE Program says only that the flaw was published) and `source_in_window` (is the matter inside the lookback of its category?). It is told to answer true when it cannot tell, because a finding that is dropped by mistake is lost.
- **A false answer drops the finding.** It is not stored. The [trace](../infrastructure/web-research.md#the-run-trace) counts it in `dropped` (`verifier.other_company`, `verifier.not_supported`, `verifier.out_of_window`; a finding that fails several questions is counted once, under the first in that order) and keeps a rejection (`stage: "verifier"`) with the start of the claim. A category whose candidates were all taken away ends like one whose candidates were already known: "Possible risks were found but could not be verified or are already recorded", unless the caps also cut a risk of that category, which gives "More risks were found than one run keeps".
- **Only an answer takes a finding away.** A finding with no verdict is kept. So is every finding when the call does not succeed (30 seconds, retries included; an error; an answer that does not fit the schema): the call is recorded as failed (`purpose: "verifier"`, `ok: false`, the tokens it used) without the message of its error, which can quote the request or hold a key. The verifier never fails a run.
- **Off unless asked for.** `analyse` and `runVendorResearch` run it only with `verifier: true`, as `gather` and `perCategory` are on only when asked for. The job and live evaluations turn it on. A case of the evaluation can replay it (`verifier` in the case and in the recording; the case `verifier-drops-findings` holds a finding about another company, one whose quote says less than its claim and one that is too old).

### Credibility

Every finding that stays gets a credibility label, `low`, `medium` or `high`, computed by code from the sources it cites (`credibilityOf` in `credibility.ts`, called by `analyse`); every run stores it and the screen shows it in place of the confidence that earlier runs showed. No model sees, writes or decides it. It says how well the sources establish the facts, not how likely or how serious the risk is. The design is in sections D3 (the registry) and D5 (the rule) of `docs/superpowers/specs/2026-10-29-vendor-risk-database-design.md`.

- **The source registry.** `sourceRegistry/entries.ts` is version `sr-6` (`REGISTRY_VERSION`): 119 entries, 25 official (governments, regulators, courts, official databases), 78 trusted press and 16 unverified. A source falls in one of four categories: `official`, `trusted_press`, `vendor` (the vendor speaking for itself) and `unverified` (everything else, a host the registry does not know included). An entry names its hosts, or a suffix, with the reason, the evidence, the day it was reviewed and the paths that downgrade it (`DowngradeRule`: a segment, the first segments, the first segments with `#` for digits, a slug, or a host); there is no entry that matches by a pattern. sr-2 added the answers of Q25 and Q26: the forums and communities of G11 and the platforms where users publish (G6–G9) are user content, and the documents that others file with an authority are unverified on an official entry (EDGAR filings under `/Archives`, public comments on regulations.gov, RECAP documents on CourtListener); sr-3 added their other addresses (EDGAR's viewers `/ix`, `/ixviewer` and `/cgi-bin/viewer`, the download host `downloads.regulations.gov`, CourtListener's document pages `/docket/<id>/<number>/…`); sr-4 (M2) made an EDGAR filing under the CIK a person stored for the vendor the vendor's own word, in the archive and in the viewers. `sr-5` is one version for four decisions of 2026-10-31 and 2026-11-01. **Q8, government domains**: the entry that matched any `gov`, `govt`, `gouv`, `gob` or `gv` label before a country code by a regular expression is gone, since 87% of the 1,240 names it matched were ordinary registrations, and a government domain is official only when a page of its registry, its regulator or its holder was read. `.mil`, gov.br, go.id, go.th, go.tz, gov.sg, gov.my, gov.pk, gob.pk, gov.lk, gov.za, gov.pl, gov.hk, gob.gt, gc.ca and gov.de are official entries with the page that supports each read; bund.de, gov.fi and gov.co are official entries whose operators' own pages were read but do not state one of the criteria (evidence status P); govt.nz, go.ke, go.ug, gov.ug and gov.ie have no entry, so they are unverified until a person reads their rule. **Q9, trusted press, under a ruling on evidence**: a page counts as evidence only when its site's `robots.txt` does not bar Anthropic's agents. The entries of the BBC, The Guardian, USA Today, CNBC, the Sydney Morning Herald, Bloomberg, The Washington Post, Forbes, CBC, Dark Reading and SC Media took the reason, the evidence and the paths that downgrade sponsored, advisory or council content that Q9 gives them (the Guardian's evidence is its editorial code alone, since theguardian.com bars Claude-User); The Hacker News is unverified, since no standards or corrections page of it was seen; the other publishers stay as sr-4 had them. **Rule V** was widened (below). **Q28** is in the classifier (below). `sr-6` (2026-11-05) adds six entries for the hosts of the AI databases (Q30), reviewed on that day with evidence status P: `incidentdatabase.ai` (`ai-incident-database`), `aiaaic.org` (`aiaaic`), `oecd.ai` (`oecd-ai`), `avidml.org` (`avid`) and `airisk.mit.edu` (`mit-ai-incident-tracker`) are unverified, each with its reason, and `atlas.mitre.org` (`mitre-atlas`, publisher MITRE ATLAS) is trusted press, because MITRE curates the ATLAS case studies from published research and incident reports with references for each. No data is copied for the five unverified hosts: a host the registry does not know is unverified and unreviewed already, so their entries only replace the mark "unreviewed" on a page that a web search cites with a reason. Each entry names the host of the database and not its owner's (`mitre.org`, `mit.edu` and `oecd.org` stay unknown), and the `oecd.ai` entry covers every page of its host, the OECD's own policy pages included, so it has to be revisited if a finding ever needs them. There is no entry for GW DAIL (a blog under `gwu.edu`: an entry would label the whole university) or for the Agent Incident Registry (its host was not verified), and `nhtsa.gov` and `fda.gov` are official already under the `.gov` entry. The six entries have no named review owner yet (Q18 of the spec is open); the test of the registry lists them as due from 2027-11-05 and fails on them from 2028-05-05. Every change is a pull request with a reason, evidence, a review date and a new version; the test of the registry names an entry reviewed 12 months ago and fails on one reviewed 18 months ago.
- **The classifier** (`sourceRegistry/classify.ts`) decides the category of a cited address in this order: user content is `unverified` (Q25: a forum or community section or host of G11 on any site, the vendor's own included, and a platform of G6–G9 unless the page is on the vendor's own subdomain of it, such as `acme.substack.com`); the vendor's own site is `vendor`; the host is normalised; the longest host entry is taken, then the longest suffix; a blocked entry; a downgrade (the paths of the entry, and the opinion, paid and press-release segments and the `sponsored.`, `paidpost.` and `partners.` hosts of any trusted publisher); a syndicated copy, credited to the publisher that its canonical link names (one level deep, never the vendor, an unknown host or another copy) or to its issuer; a paid wire, `vendor` when its text carries an e-mail address on the vendor's domain or a subdomain of it (Q27, `emailOnDomain`) and `unverified` otherwise; else the category of the entry. A filing in the EDGAR archive or viewer under the CIK stored for the vendor is `vendor` only when the filing's header names that CIK as a filer, a person it is filed by or a reporting owner (Q28, `edgarFiler.ts`: `filerRoleOf` answers `vendor`, `other` or no answer); with `other` or no answer it falls to the Q26 downgrade, `unverified`, for the archive path and the viewer forms alike. A record of a public database is classified by its database (`classifyRecord`, `recordMatchOf`; a record of the connector's SEC read-through keeps its strength, which does not depend on who filed): the catalog of exploited vulnerabilities and the NVD are official with a strong match (they name the vendor's product), the SEC and the three sanctions lists official with a weak one (found by the name alone), the Consolidated Screening List official with a weak one too (an entry of an OFAC list that carries the vendor's verified website or its LEI is strong), NHTSA's crash reports, the FDA list of AI-enabled devices and the FDA recalls of those devices official with a weak one too, MITRE ATLAS trusted press with a weak one (MITRE curates the case studies with references, but it is not an authority, and the match is by name), the AI Incident Database unverified with a weak one (an aggregator of allegations), Have I Been Pwned unverified, and the status page is the vendor's. The 23 lists of 2026-11-10 are classified the same way (`RECORD_CLASSES`; the registry stays `sr-6`): sixteen are official with a weak match (Canada's sanctions list, the California breach list, the UK OFSI, CMA, MHRA and ATRS lists, the FTC penalty and nonmerger lists, the EU competition cases, the Colombian sanctions, the Canadian and US federal AI registers, the Dutch register, NHTSA's recalls and investigations and the FedRAMP Marketplace), four are trusted press with a weak one (the AI Hallucination Cases database, the Foundation Model Transparency Index, Epoch AI's data and the C2PA conforming products list), two are unverified with a weak one (the PipeRoll registry, which one editor keeps, and the disclose.io list of community contributions), and GLEIF is trusted press with a strong one, since it is matched by the LEI. A record of the vendor risk database carries the strength of its own match instead, so a UK entry found by the vendor's verified website is strong.
- **Rule V.** A source of the vendor is a statement when the finding is in an event category (`security_incidents`, `availability`, `legal_regulatory`, `financial_health`), when it is the status page, or when it is a page of the vendor's site whose first section, after a locale (and after `intl` and a locale, as in `/intl/en-us/legal`) or after one of the parent words `company`, `about`, `about-us` or `corporate`, is a trust, security, legal, privacy, terms, policy, compliance, subprocessor or status section (the run-together names `privacypolicy`, `termsofservice`, `termsofuse` and `website-terms` among them), or whose host begins with `trust.`, `security.` or `status.` (`isStatementPage`, versioned with the registry). A page with a blog, news, newsroom, press or media section never is, at any depth, and the title of a page is not read. Anywhere else it is a self-claim, which never raises a label.
- **The rule.** A finding whose quote the snippet check did not find on the page is `low` (`contradicted`). Of the other sources, a record that its database withdrew, rejected or delisted supports nothing (the vendor risk database offers a run only the records its lists still hold, so a cited record leaves `active` only later, by the status job, see [Vendor risk database](#vendor-risk-database)), and a record found by name only supports `low` at most. A record of a list that gives context only (`CONTEXT_ONLY_RECORD_SOURCES`, ten lists: see **The third round of lists** above) supports nothing either: it is left out of the supporting sources before any rule is applied, so that a strong FedRAMP record beside a KEV record leaves the label to the KEV record, a strong GLEIF record is no trusted publisher, and a finding that cites nothing but such records gets the last rule, `low` by `unverified`, withdrawn or not (the rule `inactive` looks at the other sources only); the note `name_only` is still set from every cited record, so a weak record of such a list beside a strong KEV record leaves the label to the KEV record and keeps the note. The label is then `high` by the first of `official_record` (an official record matched strongly), `official_page`, `vendor_statement` and `trusted_press_two` (trusted press of two registry entries; one entry is one publisher) that holds with a source that was read. When one of them holds only with search results that nobody could confirm on a page, the label is capped at `medium` (`capped_unconfirmed`). Then `medium` for trusted press of exactly one publisher (`trusted_press_one`), counting the strong sources only: a weak record is no publisher for this rule either, so a case study of MITRE ATLAS, whose class is trusted press but whose match is always by name, never makes a label `medium`, and a finding whose only trusted-press source is such a record is `low` (`name_only`). Otherwise the label is `low` (`name_only`, `unverified`, `self_claim`, and `inactive` for a cited record that its list no longer stands by, a record of a list that gives context only apart). The note `name_only` ("this may be another company") is set whenever a cited record matched by name only, whatever the label.
- **The basis.** With the label comes the reason for it (`CredibilityBasis` in `i.vendorResearch.ts`): the rule, the version of the registry, the note, and each cited source as the rule saw it (its category, publisher, registry entry and reading, the vendor's kind of word, the database, match and status of a record and, for a record of the vendor risk database, its id in the list, the day of the copy and whether the copy was stale, or, once the status job changed its status, the day of the change and the list that rejected its CVE when that is another, `rejected_by`, and the downgraded, syndicated and blocked marks).
- **There is one set of rules.** The vendor risk database has no flag, so every run is rated by the credibility rules, and the earlier path of the model's confidence is gone: the analysis asks with `CREDIBILITY_PROMPT_VERSION` (`vr-20`), whose prompt leaves out the confidence field and its rule, reads a source as a record or a change only by the `database` and `change` attributes that code writes from its provenance, words the databases (the CVE Program beside the NVD, most of whose flaws are not exploited, and since `vr-17` a filing of the SEC as the vendor's own only when its record has no lookup note) and the six sanctions and screening lists, the UN's, Canada's and the US Consolidated Screening List's among them (an entry matched by name only is written "An entity named … is on …", and one matched by the vendor's verified website or one of its identifiers, a registration number or an LEI, is about the vendor unless the identifiers it lists say otherwise, and a designation in force is a risk in legal and regulatory matters; a listing on the Entity List or the Military End User List is an export licence requirement and not a sanction), words the AI databases (an entry of them is matched by name only and is written "An entity named … appears in …", with at most one risk for each of the AI Incident Database, MITRE ATLAS, NHTSA and the FDA recalls, in AI-specific risks, a likelihood of Possible at most and an action plan that checks who the vendor is; an entry of the FDA list raises no risk; a recall is not a finding that the AI failed, and the recalling firm may be another company than the vendor), words the lists of 2026-11-10 by their class (an entry of an AI incident, enforcement and case, product safety or data breach list raises at most one risk, and an entry of a register or status list none), and whose schema has no confidence field. The research reads the news in the order of the registry's categories, and a source of a blocked registry entry is dropped from the evidence pack before the analysis (`buildEvidencePack(entries, { dropBlocked })`, counted as `source.blocked` in the trace). Before the labels are worked out, the analysis asks who filed each EDGAR filing that a kept finding cites under the vendor's stored CIK (Q28; at most 5 headers a run, with the SEC User-Agent and a turn of the SEC spacer; see [web-research](../infrastructure/web-research.md#records-of-public-databases)). A run from before the flag was removed may hold the model's confidence and no label; the reads give it its confidence as its label until the backfill.
- **Stored.** `withCredibility` (`pipeline.ts`) gives each kept finding `credibility` and `credibility_basis`, and each of its sources the category, publisher, flags and record (`source_id`, `record_id`, `status`, `match_strength`) that the basis gives it; the columns are in `20261030223852-vendor-finding-credibility.js`, which also lets `confidence` be null for the milestone that drops it.
- **The line.** The rationale of a label is one line written by code from the basis, never by a model (`credibilityRationale` in `credibilityText.ts`): the sentence of the rule that decided it ("High: CISA Known Exploited Vulnerabilities lists it (CVE-…), as of 7 Oct 2026.", one sentence for each of the eleven rules), then, when a cited copy of the vendor risk database was stale, "The source has not been updated since {date}.", then the name-only note, "Matched by name only: this may be another company.", when the rule is not already `name_only`. A record of a list that gives context only never shapes the line: a finding that cites nothing else reads "Low: only registers that list the vendor are cited, and a register entry is not evidence of a risk.", the stale sentence and the sentence of a withdrawn record leave such records out, and the name-only note counts them like any other. The API adds this English line to every finding (`credibility_rationale`); the client words the same line from the basis in the person's language (see [Vendor research](#vendor-research)).
- **Evaluation.** Each expected finding of the replay cases names the label it should get, every case holds `maxMislabelled: 0`, eight cases are worked cases of the rule, nine more replay copies of the lists through the lookup's own `offerRecords` (a withdrawn CVE, a delisted sanctions entry, a stale copy, one name on two lists, an entry whose website is a page on the platform that holds the vendor's own page, `platform-website`, a KEV entry whose CVE the CVE Program rejected, offered by no list, `rejected-cve-mirror`, a UK entry with the vendor's name and registration number, High, `identifier-match`, a CVE that the NVD answered at run time, High as of the day of the answer, `nvd-read-through`, and an EU entry that carries the vendor's LEI under another name, High, `lei-match-other-name`), (see [Evaluating the agent](../infrastructure/web-research.md#evaluating-the-agent); the replay answers the one set of rules, and the scorer has no `overconfident` measure any more).

### Backfill of the label (M3)

A finding of a run from before the credibility rules has a confidence and no label (`credibility` is null). Until the backfill gives it one, the reads show that confidence as its label: a finding with a confidence and no label reads as that level on the basis `{ "rule": "legacy_confidence" }` (`findingColumn` in `utils/vendorResearch.utils.ts`: `COALESCE(credibility, confidence)`), and the API words a pending one "Rated before the source rules; this is the earlier confidence level until the label is recomputed." (`credibility_rationale`) and an accepted or dismissed one "Rated with the earlier confidence rule.", because nothing ever relabels a decided finding (`withRationale` in `controllers/vendorResearch.ctrl.ts`). The findings section does not show that sentence: it shows the label with an asterisk and counts the sources of the finding, and says once above the list what the asterisk stands for (see [Vendor research](#vendor-research)). The backfill gives such a finding its own label, once for each deployment, before the follow-up release drops the `confidence` column (Part B2 of `docs/superpowers/plans/2026-10-31-vendor-risk-database-m3.md`, on `feature/vendor-finding-drop-confidence`). Nothing runs it at startup or in a migration: a maintainer runs `Servers/scripts/vendorResearchBackfillCredibility.ts`. The decision is `services/vendorResearch/backfill.ts` (pure), the queries are `utils/vendorResearchBackfill.utils.ts`.

- **A pending finding** is decided by the rule's own parts (`describeSources` and `decideCredibility` in `credibility.ts`), from what its run stored: the run's `evidence` (the pack its analysis read), the run's `trace`, and the vendor as it is now (`buildTarget`). A cited address that the stored pack lacks reads as unconfirmed, so a run that kept no evidence gives at most Medium (`capped_unconfirmed`). The label is stored with the basis under the rule's backfill name, `backfill_<rule>` (`backfill_official_record`, `BackfilledCredibilityBasis`), and the line is the rule's sentence followed by "Rated again with the current rule." (the screen adds that note only in an open row) The backfill writes the label and its basis only, so the finding's `sources` stay as its run stored them, without categories; the screen takes each source's tag from the basis, from the source at the same place with the same address (`categoryOf` in `FindingRow.tsx`).
- **An accepted or dismissed finding** keeps the confidence the person decided it by: `credibility = confidence` and the basis `{ "rule": "legacy_confidence" }` (`LegacyCredibilityBasis`); the API's line is "Rated with the earlier confidence rule." (above). No screen shows that line for a decided finding today: the Research findings tab lists pending findings only, and the API returns decided ones with `status=accepted` or `status=dismissed`. A decided finding without a confidence is left as it is. `StoredCredibilityBasis` (the rule's basis, a backfilled one or a legacy one) types the column on the server and in the client, which words both notes in German, French and Spanish too. The client's `credibilityRationale` (`Clients/src/application/utils/vendorResearchText.ts`) takes the finding's status as well: for an accepted or dismissed finding a `legacy_confidence` label reads "Rated with the earlier confidence rule.", and a pending finding, or a call with no status, keeps the sentence that ends "until the label is recomputed". The findings section words a legacy label itself (the label with an asterisk, its sources and the legend) and shows neither line.
- **The records by their addresses.** The per-run lookups kept no provenance, so the addresses they cited stand for it (`provenanceByAddress`, only these exact `https` forms, at most 2,048 characters): `https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/<CVE>` is an NVD record, strong (the per-run KEV records cited the same page); `https://sanctionssearch.ofac.treas.gov/Details.aspx?id=<n>` an OFAC entry, weak, with the name-only note; `https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/<cik>/<accession>/<document>`, whose `<accession>` folder has 18 digits, an SEC filing, strong only when the CIK stored on the vendor is `<cik>` as a number, weak otherwise; `https://haveibeenpwned.com/api/v3/breach/<name>` a Have I Been Pwned breach, with no match; `https://<host>/history` and `https://<host>/incidents/<id>` a status page record, the vendor's statement, when `<host>` is one the status page lookup asks for the vendor's domain (`statusHostsOf`): its own status host (`status.<domain>`) whoever read the page, and the brand's hosts (`<brand>.statuspage.io`, `<brand>status.com`), which could be another company's, only when the stored pack holds the address. A lookup put its records into the ledger before the research, as pages under its database's category (`RECORD_CATEGORY`: `security_incidents` for the NVD, the SEC and Have I Been Pwned, `legal_regulatory` for OFAC, `availability` for the status page), and the ledger holds an address once: when the stored pack holds the address as anything else, under another category or as a search result, the research found the page itself, and it is read as any page, as at run time (an EDGAR filing found under certifications is read as any page; the backfill makes no request, so no filer of a filing under the stored CIK is known and it reads as `unverified`, as the rule of Q28 says). Each such record is `active`, its id is the CVE, the OFAC id, the accession number with its dashes (`0000320193-26-000001`, as the SEC writes it and the live SEC records carry it) or the breach name (a status page record has none, as at run time), and its `as_of` is the day of the finding (UTC), the day the lookup read it.
- **The verdict of the snippet check**, from the run's trace first (`verdictOf`): the steps `by: "verification"`, `tool: "read"` whose address is one the finding cites and whose category is the finding's or absent. A finding that cites a page the run read (the stored pack holds the address as `kind: "page"`) has no verdict, as at run time, where the check examines no such finding: a step of one of its addresses was another finding's, since the trace keeps no finding id. For a snippet-only finding, a check that found the quote off the page makes it `contradicted`; any other check makes it unread; with no check in the trace, the spec's rule decides (a stored `low` was contradicted at its first cited address, any other was unread). For another finding, a check that confirmed the quote makes that source read.
- **The status job** never decides a backfilled or legacy label again (`relabelFinding` skips a rule that is not one of the eleven): its sources have no record of the vendor risk database to follow.
